FOREWORD HONORABLE MURRAY HULBERT 

Judge of the United States District Court for the 

Southern District of New York 

The underlying purpose of all of our Bankruptcy Acts has been to afford the means whereby honest debtors might free themselves of overwhelming financial burdens with some hope of future rehabilitation. The only condition imposed on these debtors is that they must surrender all of their assets for division among their creditors in accordance with the priority of such creditors as between themselves and after payment of necessary administration expenses. 

Unfortunately, the administration of previous Bankruptcy laws has led many to wonder how the purpose and intent of the lawmakers could be so at variance with the results actually accomplished. It is hardly necessary to recall the abuse connected with our late financial depression which led the public to believe that bankruptcies were for the benefit of various lawyers, accountants and members of so-called "protective" committees, rather than for the benefit of the creditors whose money was involved.

The new Bankruptcy Act, popularly known as "The Chandler Act" was designed to correct these abuses and to return the administration of bankrupt estates to the hands of interested creditors under the supervision of specially qualified officers designated by the Federal Court. 

The consequent gain to individual creditors in increased dividends, as well as the resultant gain to the community in salvaging businesses where failure arose from fundamental business conditions rather than individual weakness, is obvious. Business men, or creditors, and lawyers not especially trained in bankruptcy work must, of course, take their new responsibilities seriously else either the old abuses will reappear or bankruptcy administration will drift into the ever widening realm where justice is dispensed by administrative tribunal. iii

The present book seems to be adapted to serve a most useful purpose in assisting business men and the average lawyer to readily acquaint themselves with the revised law and procedure. It is not only concise, but is written in language easily understandable to laymen as opposed to the mystifying legal terms sometimes employed by text writers. 

Notably, it contains a carefully prepared and exhaustive index which will be of inestimable value in more quickly obtaining the correct answer to an ordinary problem. 

New York, N. Y.

October 20, 1941. 

MURRAY HULBERT

BANKRUPTCY CHAPTER I ORIGIN, NATURE AND PURPOSE OF BANKRUPTCY 

Origin and development of bankruptcy laws. It has been said that while insolvency is as old as commerce itself, the concept of bankruptcy-a haven of refuge from the slings and arrows of financial misfortune-is comparatively modern. 

The statement, however, is not entirely true. The social need for providing relief to unfortunate debtors is scarcely a modern concept. 

Over 4000 years ago (about 2300 B.C.) the Babylonian code of Hammurabi contained the following provision: "If anyone owe a debt for a loan, and the storm prostrate the grain, or the harvest fail, or the grain grow not for lack of water, in that year, he need not give his creditor any grain; he washes his debt tablet and pays no rent for such year." 

The Tribes of Israel recognized the same social need in the custom of cancelling debts every seven years. 

The history of Greece affords similar evidence. When Solon swept away the mortgage burden that lay like a pall over most of the land of Greece, when he freed the wretches who languished in slavery for debt, and forbade contracts that put into bondage not only the debtor himself but his children and dependents as well, he gave eloquent proof that even in ancient times the more enlightened rulers have recognized that financial misfortune, like physical misfortune, is something that ought to be cured rather than punished.

But the law has not always been tolerant of financial misfortune. Ancient Roman law treated insolvency as a crime punish- 1

BANKRUPTCY 

able by imprisonment, servitude and even death. Relief was afforded by the Justinian Code which provided exemption to a debtor upon surrender of his goods (cessio bonorum). In England, the debtors' gaol was an established institution until Parliament in the 18th century offered the debtor escape from prison upon the same principle as that recognized in the Justinian Code: surrender of one's assets for the benefit of one's creditors,-with the understanding, however, that this would not discharge the debtor as to any unpaid deficiency.

Bankruptcy laws in the United States. In the United States, also, the original approach to insolvency was punitive. As late as 1840 men were imprisoned for debt in some of our states. 

However, even during the colonial period the legal principle developed that imprisonment for debt was not wholly equitable, and that if one surrendered all his worldly goods, he ought to be relieved of all his debts. By the time the United States Constitution was adopted a number of state statutes not only permitted, but compelled an insolvent debtor to surrender his assets for the benefit of his creditors.

The United States Constitution took all bankruptcy jurisdiction away from the states: first, by prohibiting any state from passing laws impairing the obligation of contracts; and second, by vesting all bankruptcy jurisdiction in Congress, which was empowered to pass "uniform laws on the subject of bankruptcies throughout the United States."

Under the exclusive power thus vested in Congress, the first Bankruptcy Act was passed in 1800. It was designed primarily for creditors. Its principal object was to secure an equal distribution of the debtor's assets. The Act was not a success, and was repealed in 1803.

The second Bankruptcy Act, passed in 1841 after the panic of 1837, and intended to appease the unfortunate debtor, introduced the principle of voluntary bankruptcy. The Act was even shorter lived than the first one, lasting a little longer than a year. 

After the Civil War, Congress passed the Bankruptcy Act of 1867. This was followed by nine amendments, the most important of which was the amendment of 1874 which introduced the principle of compositions in bankruptcy. 

The Bankruptcy Act of 1867 was repealed in 1878. 

It was twenty years before a new act was passed, the Act of 1898. That Act functioned until several years after the financial upheaval of 1929. During this period changing economic 2

ORIGIN, NATURE AND PURPOSE OF BANKRUPTCY 

conditions, as usual, gradually outmoded the Act, until the depression which followed the panic of 1929 brought to a head the need for radical change.

Purpose of National Bankruptcy Act. From the nature and origin of bankruptcy legislation in this country, it will be observed that the essential purpose of the National Bankruptcy 

Act is twofold: (1) To give all creditors an equal opportunity to share in the debtor's property in proportion to their claims. The Act permits an orderly, fair and equitable distribution of a bankrupt's property. Creditors are not compelled to compete in a mad rush to beat one another to the bankrupt's property by judgments, executions, levies, attachments, etc., and to spend their substance against one another in a series of lawsuits to establish priority in reaching the debtor's property. (2) To give honest but insolvent debtors an opportunity to make a new start in life. If an insolvent debtor were faced with a bleak future, wherein his labours would be mortgaged indefinitely, no incentive would remain toward productive effort. Under the National Bankruptcy Act an insolvent debtor, if he has been honest, may get a fresh start by surrendering all his property to his creditors. The recent trend of bankruptcy legislation is to broaden this purpose.

Provisions for relief of debtors. The economic depression which followed the financial crash of 1929 revealed the necessity for lightening the burden of debtors stricken by abnormal conditions. In 1933 Congress added a new chapter, Chapter VIII, to the Bankruptcy Act of 1898, making provision for the relief of debtors. Section 74 of Chapter VIII gave debtors an opportunity to work their way out of their financial difficulties by judicially controlled compositions or extension agreements. The idea was to give debtors a new business start without the stigma of bankruptcy. Section 75 gave farmers a similar opportunity. 

Section 77 provided machinery whereby railroads engaged in interstate commerce could effect reorganizations under judicial direction.

In 1934 Congress extended the relief provisions of Chapter 

VIII by adding two new sections, 77A and 77B. The latter section provided for corporate reorganizations. 

Extension of debtor relief: the Chandler Act. The amend ments of 1933 and 1934 proved to be but temporary makeshifts. In 1938 a new act-the Chandler Act-radically amended the Bankruptcy Act of 1898 and superseded the amendments

BANKRUPTCY 

of 1933 and 1934. Broadly, the objects sought to be achieved by the act of 1938 were as follows: (1) (2) (3) (4) (5) 

To simplify and improve the procedure whereby honest but insolvent debtors may secure relief without incurring the stigma of bankruptcy, and to extend the relief to a wider class of debtors. 

To improve the procedure and practice relating to corporate reorganizations. 

To strengthen creditor-control of bankrupt estates in various ways, such as by provision for official creditors' committees, instead of allowing bankruptcy proceedings to be dominated, to the detriment of a majority of the creditors, by hand-picked groups of small creditors of questionable origin and standing.

To improve the administration of bankrupt estates generally, by a series of miscellaneous amendments designed to achieve more efficient and economical administration.

To minimize frauds and evasions of bankrupts. 

By the 1938 amendments to the Bankruptcy Act, sections 75 and 77 (dealing with agricultural compositions and railroad reorganizations respectively) were retained in Chapter VIII. By a series of new chapters, the debtor-relief principle was extended on a broader basis. 

Local taxing agencies. Extension of the debtor-relief principle to local taxing agencies is embodied in Chapter IX, affording plans of composition for such bodies. 

Corporate reorganizations. Provisions for corporate reorganization under section 77B of the old Act are superseded by new provisions embodied in Chapter X of the new Act. 

Relief to ordinary debtors: “arrangements.” The former provisions for relief to ordinary debtors are extended in a new Chapter XI entitled, "Arrangements."

Real property arrangements. Chapter XII of the new Act provides for compositions and extensions affecting real property owners.

Wage earners' plans. Debtor-relief to wage earners is afforded by Chapter XIII of the new Act.

ORIGIN, NATURE AND PURPOSE OF BANKRUPTCY 

Corporate reorganizations and arrangements will be more fully discussed in subsequent chapters. 

Bankruptcy and insolvency distinguished. Bankruptcy is a legal status, insolvency a financial one. 

A person is not really a bankrupt unless he has been so declared ("adjudicated") in a bankruptcy court. The National 

Bankruptcy Act, however, includes in its definition of a bankrupt, the following persons: (a) A person who has filed a voluntary petition in bankruptcy; (b) A person against whom an involuntary petition has been filed; and (c) A person who has been adjudged a bankrupt; (d) A person who has been discharged in bankruptcy, but against whom an application to revoke the discharge is pending.

Insolvency, on the other hand, may exist regardless of legal proceedings. It relates to a person's financial condition. The term has two meanings, one in the state or equity sense, and one in the Federal or bankruptcy sense. 

Insolvency in the equity sense. Insolvency in the state or equity sense of the term means inability to meet one's debts as they mature. One may be "worth" a million dollars in frozen assets, yet be insolvent in the equity sense because of inability to meet a $5,000 note when it falls due. 

Insolvency in the bankruptcy sense. Under section 1 of the

Bankruptcy Act, a person is declared to be insolvent if the aggregate value of his assets, at a fair valuation, is insufficient to pay his debts. Under the new amendments to the Bankruptcy Act adopted in 1938, however, the definition of insolvency in the equity sense is also recognized; first, under the section which specifies, as an additional act of bankruptcy, the appointment of a receiver or trustee due to a debtor's inability to pay his debts when due, and second, in connection with "arrangements," more fully discussed hereafter.

CHAPTER COURTS OF BANKRUPTCY 

Federal courts have exclusive bankruptcy jurisdiction. The

Constitution of the United States gave Congress power to pass "uniform laws on the subject of bankruptcies in the United States." In framing such laws, Congress had the alternative of creating a new court to take jurisdiction of bankruptcy matters, or of entrusting such jurisdiction to existing Federal courts. It chose the latter course, and gave the Federal courts exclusive jurisdiction of all bankruptcy matters.

The particular Federal court thus chosen for exclusive bankruptcy jurisdiction is the United States District Court. This is the Federal court of original jurisdiction; that is, the Federal court where suits are commenced in the first instance. 

For administrative purposes, the United States District Court is divided into Federal districts. Each state has at least one district. Some states have several. New York, for instance, has four. 

The proceedings to have a person declared a bankrupt, and to administer his property in bankruptcy, are brought and conducted in the particular Federal district where such person has resided, is domiciled or has had his principal place of business for a period of at least six months before the proceedings are commenced.

The bankruptcy court is an equity court. Courts of bankruptcy are limited to equity jurisdiction only: they have no common law jurisdiction whatsoever. The purposes and methods of bankruptcy legislation find no counterpart in the development of the common law. They are confined to certain equitable rules and principles embodied in the Bankruptcy Act, having to do with the equitable disposition of a bankrupt's property and the equitable administration of his estate.

Summary v. plenary jurisdiction. The jurisdiction of a bank ruptcy court is said to be summary, not plenary. 

Jurisdiction is summary when a court may make a judgment or order in a particular proceeding forthwith, without the ceremony or delay of a trial. In a bankruptcy proceeding, the inquiry does not involve the trial of conflicting issues between different parties to a suit. It involves, rather, an inquiry into the af-

COURTS OF BANKRUPTCY 

fairs of a single person or firm, so as to determine, in the first place, whether such person or firm is bankrupt, and, if so, what should be done about assembling and administering his assets and discharging his debts. Where, upon such inquiry, it is necessary to determine a disputed issue, such as the issue as to whether the alleged bankrupt is insolvent, the court may order a trial before a jury; but in all other respects, the court may decide all bankruptcy questions upon a hearing before it, simply and promptlythat is, "summarily.'

A plenary suit, on the other hand, involves a full, complete trial of conflicting issues between adverse parties. If a bankrupt estate has any disputed claim against an outside party, or vice versa, the trustee, acting on behalf of the creditors, may sue or be sued in another court for a judgment in respect of such claim. 

For example, suppose John Jones makes a contract with

William Smith. Later, Jones is adjudicated a bankrupt. Smith breached the contract, for which Jones might have sued him had be not gone into bankruptcy. The trustee on behalf of the creditors in the Estate of John Jones, Bankrupt, may now step forward and bring a plenary suit in the state court against William 

Smith for breach of contract.

On the other hand, if there is no real, substantial basis to an adverse claim, that is, if a third party contests a claim by the estate, but such contest is clearly without foundation and involves no reasonable room for doubt or controversy, the bankruptcy court may decide the matter summarily, without permitting or directing a plenary suit.

Suppose, for example, that the Estate of John Jones has a claim against William Smith for property alleged to have been fraudulently transferred from John Jones to William Smith. If the bankruptcy court, upon a preliminary inquiry, decides that the case is clear and that Smith's defense is palpably without foundation, it will summarily order the property to be turned over to the estate. Such a proceeding is sometimes referred to as a turnover proceeding. If, on the other hand, there appears to be a real question as to whether the transfer was fraudulent or not, the bankruptcy court will not proceed to weigh the evidence in order to determine such question, but will allow the question to be decided in a plenary suit. In re Farrow, 13 Fed. (2d) 261 (D. C., Mass.).

Officers of bankruptcy court. The judicial officers of a bankruptcy court are the judge and referee. The judge is the ultimate

BANKRUPTCY 

authority on matters of original jurisdiction, including matters not only brought up directly before him in the first instance, but also matters on appeal from the referee. The appellate jurisdiction of the district judge is comparatively new, and is the result of the enlarged judicial powers recently granted to the referee. The judge may review all orders and decrees made by the referee. On the other hand, the orders of the district judge in all bankruptcy proceedings may be reviewed on appeal by the United States Circuit Court of Appeals, and the orders, judgments and decrees of the latter court are subject to further review by the 

United States Supreme Court. 

Powers of referee. Under the recent amendments to the 

Bankruptcy Act, the powers of referees in bankruptcy have been greatly enlarged. After a bankruptcy case has been referred to a referee for administration of the bankrupt estate, the referee is, to all intents and purposes, the court of bankruptcy, limited only by the provisions for review, and by the provisions which specifically call for action by the judge himself. The referee now has power to adjudicate or dismiss a petition in bankruptcy, to grant, deny or revoke a discharge, and to confirm, refuse to confirm or set aside an "arrangement".

A referee cannot, however, commit for contempt, but he may certify to a judge acts which constitute contempt in proceedings before the referee, and for such acts the judge may punish for contempt. The Bankruptcy Act (sec. 41) provides: "A person shall not, in proceedings before a referee, (1) disobey or resist any lawful order, process, or writ; (2) misbehave during a hearing or so near the place thereof as to obstruct the same; (3) neglect to produce, after having been ordered to do so, any pertinent document; or (4) refuse to appear after having been subpoenaed, or, upon appearing, refuse to take the oath as a witness, or, having taken the oath, refuse to be examined according to law: Provided, That a person other than a bankrupt or, where the bankrupt is a corporation, its officers, or the members of the board of directors or trustees or of other similar controlling bodies, shall not be required to attend as a witness before a referee at a place more than one hundred miles from such person's place of residence, or unless his lawful mileage and fee for one day's attendance, shall be first paid or tendered to him *** The referee shall forthwith certify the facts to the judge, if any person shall do any of the things forbidden in this section, and he may serve or cause to be served upon such person an order requiring such per-

COURTS OF BANKRUPTCY 

son to appear before the judge upon a day certain to show cause why he should not be adjudged in contempt by reason of the facts so certified. The judge shall thereupon, in a summary manner, hear the evidence as to the acts complained of, and, if it is such as to warrant him in so doing, punish such person in the same manner and to the same extent as for a contempt committed before him or commit such person upon the same conditions as if the doing of the forbidden act had occurred with reference to the process of the court of bankruptcy, or in the presence of the judge."

Duties of referee. A referee's duties are not only judicial but administrative as well. Among the duties of a referee are: (1) To examine all schedules of property and lists of creditors filed by bankrupts and cause corrections and amendments thereto.

To furnish information concerning bankrupt estates to all parties in interest. (2) (3) 

To give notices to creditors. (4) 

To make up records embodying facts, etc. (5) (6) (7) 

To conduct hearings.

To keep and transmit to the clerks the records of all proceedings involving the administration of bankrupt estates. 

To declare dividends.

Trustees. Laymen sometimes confuse the offices, powers and duties of a referee with those of a trustee. Although both referees and trustees are officers of the court, a referee functions primarily as the court itself, whereas a trustee functions primarily on behalf of the creditors. The functions and duties of a referee are primarily judicial; those of a trustee are purely administrative, the trustee being really the representative of the creditors. The trustee's duties are more fully discussed in a subsequent chapter dealing with the administration of a bankrupt estate. 

Receivers and marshals. In exceptional cases, it may be necessary to entrust the property of a bankrupt to a receiver or marshal for purposes of custody and safe keeping, pending a determination by the court as to whether a petition should be dismissed or an adjudication ordered, and in the latter case, until a trustee can be elected. The duties of receivers and marshals will be discussed in a subsequent chapter dealing with the custody of a bankrupt's property.

CHAPTER PETITIONS IN BANKRUPTCY 

In general. Bankruptcy proceedings are commenced by the filing of a petition, asking that the court declare or adjudge some person a bankrupt. In voluntary bankruptcy the petitioner asks that he himself be adjudged a bankrupt. Where the proceedings are involuntary, the petition seeks to have some person other than the petitioner or petitioners adjudged a bankrupt. 

Voluntary petitions: who may file. Any person, firm or corporation (except a municipal, railroad, insurance or banking corporation, or a building and loan association) may file a voluntary petition in bankruptcy.

Involuntary petitions: who may file. Three or more creditors who have provable claims against any person amounting in the aggregate (in excess of the value of any security held by them) to $500 or over, may file an involuntary petition. If the combined number of creditors is less than twelve, any one of such creditors whose claim equals $500 net, in excess of securities, may file the petition.

Involuntary petitions: against whom may be filed. Any natural person (except a wage earner or farmer) and any moneyed, business or commercial corporation (except a building and loan association, or a municipal, railroad, insurance or banking corporation) owing debts to the amount of $1,000 or over, may be forced into involuntary bankruptcy upon committing an act or acts of bankruptcy. What constitute acts of bankruptcy will be discussed in the following chapter.

A decedent's estate cannot be forced into bankruptcy. The 

Bankruptcy Act (sec. 4, subd. d), in specifying what persons may be adjudged involuntary bankrupts, says that “any natural person" may be adjudged an involuntary bankrupt. This specifically excludes decedents' estates. However, if a person dies after a petition is filed against him, his estate may be administered in bankruptcy.

Petitions by and against partnerships. Where a partnership is declared bankrupt, the separate assets of all partners, as well as the partnership estate, are drawn into bankruptcy. This applies, also, where members of a partnership, individually and as co- 10

PETITIONS IN BANKRUPTCY 

partners, are adjudged bankrupts. However, the bankruptcy of an individual partner does not draw the partnership property into his bankrupt estate, except with the consent of the remaining partners, or unless the bankrupt is the sole surviving member of the firm and has control of the partnership assets. 8 Corpus Juris 

Secundum 428-429.

The Bankruptcy Act now treats a partnership as an entity, separate from the partners. An involuntary petition may be filed against the partnership entity alone, or against it and any of the general partners. The petition may be filed either before or after dissolution of the firm, but not after a final settlement. 

Since the Bankruptcy Act treats a partnership as a separate entity, an act of bankruptcy committed by a partner individually will not be ground for a petition against the firm. 

In In re Stovall Grocery Co., 161 Fed. 882, the alleged bankrupt was a partnership doing business under the name of Stovall Grocery Company. Several acts of bankruptcy were alleged, one of which was that C. C. Stovall, a member of the firm, had conveyed and transferred his undivided half interest in a certain lot to his wife, and that such conveyance was made without consideration, and with intent to hinder, delay, and defraud his creditors and the creditors of the firm. The court said: "It will be perceived that the act of bankruptcy alleged here is the transfer by an individual member of a firm of property with intent to defraud individual creditors and firm creditors. That is not an act of bankruptcy on the part of the firm. The partnership entity must act, and what is relied upon must be its act."

However, where a petition is filed against a partnership, a trustee in bankruptcy may demand a statement of the individual assets of the partners, outside of their firm assets, even though no petition has been filed against the individual partners; and the trustee must keep separate accounts of partnership property and of the individual property of the partners. 

Where and how petitions filed. Petitions are filed in the office of the clerk of the United States District Court. If the petition is voluntary, it must be accompanied by a schedule of the petitioner's property showing the amount and kind of property, location, value, list of creditors and their residences, amounts due each, etc. The petition, whether voluntary or involuntary, must be under oath. 11

BANKRUPTCY 

Proceedings upon voluntary petition. In voluntary proceedings the bankrupt merely files a petition, accompanied by schedules of his assets and liabilities, and asks for discharge. In these schedules he lists all his assets in detail, and all his liabilities, actual or contingent. These schedules must be under oath, and must be executed in triplicate. The three copies are filed in the office of the District

Court where the bankrupt lives or has his place of business. Immediately upon filing a voluntary petition, the petitioner is adjudicated a bankrupt. The procedure subsequent to this is the same as in the case of involuntary proceedings after adjudication. 

Proceedings upon involuntary petition. Three or more creditors, if the combined number of creditors is twelve or more, or one creditor, if the combined number of creditors is less than twelve, may file a petition setting forth that within the preceding four months the alleged bankrupt has committed one or more of the acts of bankruptcy. The alleged bankrupt is served with a "writ of subpoena", which is the equivalent of a summons. Such service is returnable within ten days. The alleged bankrupt has five days after the return day within which to answer or plead under oath to the petition. If he fails to answer and the petition is in proper form, he is adjudged a bankrupt. If he denies the allegations of the petition, a hearing must be had on the issues. 

The alleged bankrupt may demand a jury where solvency is an issue. If, at the hearing, the allegations of the petition are sustained, the debtor is declared or "adjudged" a bankrupt; otherwise, the petition is dismissed. 12

CHAPTER  

In general. Regardless of a person's financial condition, he cannot be forced into bankruptcy unless he has committed one of the acts of bankruptcy. The Bankruptcy Act lists six acts of bankruptcy. A petition may be filed against a person within four months after the commission of any one of such acts. The six acts listed are as follows: 

"Acts of bankruptcy by a person shall consist of his having (1) conveyed, transferred, concealed, removed, or permitted to be concealed or removed any part of his property, with intent 52 Amendment to hinder, delay, or defraud his creditors or any of them; or (2) transferred, while insolvent, any portion of his property to one or more of his creditors with intent to prefer such creditors over his other creditors; or (3) suffered or permitted, while insolvent, any creditor to obtain a lien upon any of his property through legal proceedings and not having vacated or discharged such lien within thirty days from the date thereof or at least five days before the date set for any sale or other disposition of such property; or (4) made a general assignment for the benefit of his creditors; or (5) while insolvent or unable to pay his debts as they mature, procured, permitted, or suffered voluntarily or involuntarily the appointment of a receiver or trustee to take charge of his property; or (6) admitted in writing his inability to pay his debts and his willingness to be adjudged a bankrupt.” 

If a person 

First act of bankruptcy: fraudulent transfers. transfers or conceals any part of his property, or permits it to be transferred or concealed, with intent to "hinder, delay or defraud his creditors," he commits an act of bankruptcy. Solvency & (Bankruptcy Act definition) is a complete defense to this act. 

The practical application of this act of bankruptcy is indicated in several illustrative cases.

In In re Filer, 108 Fed. 209 (D.C., N.Y.) an insolvent debtor ran away with his assets to avoid criminal prosecution.

This was a clear case of concealing assets with intent to hinder, delay and defraud creditors.

In In re Pease, 129 Fed. 446 (D.C., Mich.) an insolvent debtor raised money to pay certain particular creditors by executing and delivering, to a security trust company, a chattel mortgage covering the debtor's entire stock in trade. The ex- 13

BANKRUPTCY 

ecution and delivery of this mortgage were held to have been in bad faith, and to have constituted an effort to hinder and delay creditors; hence, an act of bankruptcy. 

In In re Muir, 212 Fed. 495 (D.C., Pa.) a debtor in financial distress prepared a bill in equity for the appointment of a receiver. He had the pleading executed on behalf of his largest creditor, and then had it filed by his own attorney. Without being subpoenaed, he filed an answer admitting the allegations of the bill and joining in the prayer for a receiver which was set forth in the creditor's bill. The court held that while the creditor was nominally a plaintiff in the action, the application was really made by the debtor. The bill and answer both alleged solvency, but the debtor was insolvent as a matter of fact. Upon an application to have the debtor adjudged a bankrupt, the court held that the bill and answer amounted to a fraud on the court, and that the court could look beyond these pleadings to determine the issue of solvency. On the basis of the master's findings the court concluded: "We are of opinion that Muir did, by procuring the appointment of the receiver within four months preceding the filing of the petition in bankruptcy, while insolvent, make a transfer of his property with intent to hinder, delay, or defraud his creditors."

If a transfer is made without intent to defraud, the transaction does not constitute an act of bankruptcy even though it hinders and delays creditors in the collection of their claims. 

In In re Bartleson Co., 275 Fed. 390 (D.C., Fla.) a corporation conveyed real estate to a creditor within four months prior to the filing of a petition. The conveyance was made to secure past indebtedness and to obtain future advances. The evidence failed to show that the transfer was made with fraudulent design. The court therefore dismissed the petition. 

Second act of bankruptcy: giving a preference while insolvent.

A person commits an act of bankruptcy if, while insolvent, he transfers any part of his property to one or more creditors with intent to prefer them over other creditors.

Laymen frequently confuse "preferred" creditors with creditors entitled to priority, such as secured creditors. 

A preferred creditor is one who has been given a preference. 

A preference is defined by the National Bankruptcy Act (sec. 60, subd. a) as a transfer of any of the property of a debtor to a creditor, or for the benefit of a creditor, on account of some antecedent debt, made or permitted to be made by such debtor while 14

insolvent and within four months before the filing of a petition. 

For example, suppose that fifteen weeks ago, Poor, with assets of $100,000 and liabilities of $200,000, paid off a $10,000 note to Raymond and permitted the Active Corporation to procure and collect a $20,000 judgment against him. These transactions would constitute preferences. Raymond and the Active

Corporation would be preferred creditors. 

A secured creditor, on the other hand, is defined by the Bankruptcy Act (sec. 1 subd. 28) as "a creditor who has security for his debt upon the property of the bankrupt of a nature to be assignable under this Act or who owns such a debt for which some endorser, surety, or other person secondarily liable for the bankrupt has such security upon the bankrupt's assets.' 

For example, one who holds a bond of the bankrupt secured by a mortgage would be a secured creditor. 

There is nothing unfair in not disturbing the advantage possessed by a secured creditor, because his bargain with the bankrupt was based upon the security given him. But to give a preference to an unsecured creditor is to give him an advantage to which he is not entitled. It deprives other creditors of their just share, because the debtor being insolvent-there is not enough to go around for all. 

In Shingleton v. Armour Boulevard Corporation, 96 Fed. (2d) 473 (C.C.A., Mo.) a corporate debtor while insolvent, and with full knowledge of its insolvency, aided a creditor in obtaining a judgment against itself by appearing in the suit, signing a confession of judgment, and then facilitating the execution of such judgment by the sheriff. This was clearly done with intent to prefer the creditor, who had full knowledge of such intent, because she was not only one of the original incorporators, but she was also the executive secretary of the debtor corporation, and had charge of all its books, records and accounts. Within four months of the consummation of this transaction, an involuntary petition was filed against the corporation. 

The corporation opposed the petition, contending that the filing of the confession of judgment, followed by execution, did not constitute a "transfer." The lower court sustained the corporation's motion to dismiss the petition. The Circuit Court of Appeals however, reversed this order of dismissal, and in its opinion (April 22, 1938), said: "The object and purpose of the Bankruptcy Act✶✶✶ being to secure an equal and just distribution of the property of the bankrupt among creditors, to hold that an 15

BANKRUPTCY 

act of bankruptcy was not committed under the facts, as described by the amended petition, would go far to break down and destroy that purpose and object."

A preference by a solvent debtor is not unlawful. If a person is solvent, he may prefer any creditor he pleases. Other creditors cannot complain, because if they desire immediate payment, they have an adequate remedy available at law for the collection of their claims, and the debtor has assets with which to satisfy them. 

Does all this mean that when a merchant becomes insolvent, he must refrain from paying any debts whatsoever, however small? 

Suppose he owes a bill for several dollars; must he refrain from paying such a bill? This question came up in the case of In re 

Stovall Grocery Co., 161 Fed. 882, to which reference was made in the preceding chapter in connection with fraudulent transfers by an individual member of a partnership. This case alleged two acts of bankruptcy. The first of these was that the firm in question paid a note for $3 in full payment of the holder's claims, and that this payment constituted a preference, and was intended to be such. 

The court said: "I do not think that the payment of $3 to a creditor a week before the bankruptcy proceeding was instituted could be classed as a preference. It is not such a substantial transaction as would, of itself, justify the institution of a proceeding in bankruptcy How large must such a payment be before it amounts to a "substantial transaction"? The court in the Stovall case answers this question as follows: "It would be difficult to draw a line, and say what amount would be sufficient, and what would not, made in payment of a debt, to make a substantial preference. 

This would depend, more or less, on the character of the business, whether large or small." 

Third act of bankruptcy: permitting lien, while insolvent.

A person commits an act of bankruptcy if, while insolvent, he permits a creditor to obtain a lien through legal proceedings and fails to vacate or discharge the lien within thirty days, or at least within five days before the date set for any sale or other disposition of the property affected by the lien. 

This act of bankruptcy was formerly part of the second act, the statute lumping preferential transfers and preferential liens together. The two were separated as distinct acts by the amendments of 1938.

The most common lien which a creditor may be permitted to obtain through legal proceedings is by means of a judgment. 16

However, merely procuring a judgment does not necessarily constitute a lien on all of a debtor's property. In order that a judgment shall constitute a lien on a debtor's real property, it must be duly docketed or filed; and in order that it may constitute a lien against the debtor's personal property, it must as a rule be advanced to the point of issuing execution on it to a sheriff or corresponding officer. 

In In re Flushing Queensboro Laundry, 90 Fed. (2d) 601, (C.C.A., N.Y.) a judgment had been obtained against the debtor in an action brought in the municipal court. Such judgment, under the law, did not constitute a lien on the debtor's real property until a transcript of it was duly docketed in the office of the county clerk of the county where the judgment debtor's real property was located. The judgment in question had not been thus recorded. The question arose as to whether the debtor, by failing to vacate the judgment within thirty days, had committed an act of bankruptcy. The court held that it had not, since the judgment had not been docketed with the county clerk, and hence it did not create a lien against the debtor's property. 

The court further held that so far as the debtor's personal property is concerned, the judgment not only created no lien, because it had not been duly docketed, but that even if it had been docketed, it would not have constituted a lien unless and until execution on it had been issued (to a sheriff or marshal). 

If a debtor permits a creditor to obtain a lien against him in some legal proceeding, other creditors are powerless to petition the debtor into bankruptcy if they allow more than four months to pass after the lien is first obtained. For example, a person may start legal proceedings by an attachment, for the purpose of acquiring jurisdiction over the property attached, and over the owner of such property to the extent of such ownership. Such a process creates a lien. If such a lien is not vacated within thirty days from the time of its creation, the creditors are unable to seize upon such failure as act of bankruptcy, if they allow four months to elapse from the date of the attachment; and this is true even if a judgment is subsequently procured, and a petition is filed within four months after the judgment.

This situation was illustrated in the case of In re Mayhew, 31 Fed. Supp. 175, decided in the District Court of Maryland. 

Mayhew, the alleged bankrupt, had composed a song, "It's a Sin to Tell a Lie," which he had assigned against future royalties to a firm of music publishers in New York City. Mayhew being a resident of Maryland and hence a non-resident of New York, 17

BANKRUPTCY 

these royalties due him in New York were attached on February 

1. 1937, and again on June 6, 1939, in a suit brought in New

York against Mayhew, based upon plaintiff's claim to an interest in the song. On Mayhew's default, judgment was entered against him on August 11, 1939. Within four months after the entry of this judgment, namely, on November 4, 1939, a petition in bankruptcy was filed against Mayhew in the District Court of Maryland, on the ground that the New York judgment constituted a lien against Mayhew, and that he failed to vacate or discharge such lien within thirty days. On the same day that the petition was filed, a receiver was appointed in Maryland; and on November 14, 1939 the receiver was authorized to apply for ancillary receivership in the Southern District of New York, where, as stated, Mayhew had assets in the form of unpaid royalties. On November 28, 1939, Mayhew moved to dismiss the proceeding, on the ground that it did not sufficiently aver an act of bankruptcy. On December 6, 1939, the petitioning creditor was given leave to amend his petition, and the amendment was filed on December 11, 1939. On December 20, 1939, Mayhew renewed his motion to dismiss. This latter date was more than four months after the entry of the New York judgment, but within four months of the expiration of thirty days from the date of entry of said judgment. Said the court: "The judgment relied on in this case did not create the lien, which had arisen by the attachments levied more than four months prior to the bankruptcy petition. The judgment was no more than a step in the proceeding to enable the creditor to enforce the lien previously obtained. The preference thereby obtained would not be assailable in bankruptcy."

The court therefore granted Mayhew's motion to dismiss the petition, vacated the order appointing the receiver, and directed accumulated court costs to be paid by the petitioning creditor. 

Fourth act of bankruptcy: assignment for benefit of creditors.

If a person makes a general assignment for the benefit of his creditors, he commits an act of bankruptcy. Such assignments are frequently permitted by state statute. They are also frequently followed by petitions in bankruptcy, filed by creditors dissatisfied with such an assignment.

A general assignment for the benefit of creditors has been defined by the court as "a transfer by a debtor of the legal and equitable title to all his property to a trustee with authority to 18

liquidate his affairs and distribute the proceeds equitably to his creditors." Central Fibre Products Co. v. Hardin, 82 Fed. (2d) 692 (C.C.A., Tex.). 

If a debtor assigns only part of his property, this does not constitute a general assignment within the meaning of the 

Bankruptcy Act.

Neither does the transfer of corporate stock to a creditors' committee constitute a general assignment within the meaning of the Act. Such an assignment was made in the case of In re 

Bartleson Co., 275 Fed. 390 (D.C., Fla.). The assignment of stock was made to a creditors' committee under a five year voting trust agreement. By the terms of the agreement, the committee were elected directors and given complete managerial control of the company. This arrangement was followed by an involuntary petition in bankruptcy. According to the petitioning creditors, the arrangement constituted an act of bankruptcy, on the ground that it was an assignment for the benefit of creditors. The court, however, distinguished between an actual assignment of the assets themselves, and an assignment of stock for the purpose of controlling such assets. "It is true," said the court, "that the stock of the corporation was transferred to them for the purposes set out in the voting trust agreement, and, if this can be said to be a transfer of the assets of the corporation, then, and only then, could it be said that a general assignment for the benefit of the creditors had been made. As was said in one case, this action of transferring the stock and thus putting their committee in virtual charge of the corporation might be tantamount to transferring the assets, but would not constitute a general assignment for the benefit of creditors as provided in the Bankruptcy Act." 

Fifth act of bankruptcy: appointment of receiver or trustee while insolvent. If a person, either while insolvent, or while unable to pay debts as they mature, procures, permits or suffers, voluntarily or involuntarily, the appointment of a receiver or trustee to take charge of his property, he commits an act of bankruptcy.

Receivership without insolvency does not constitute an act of bankruptcy. The word "insolvency," under this specified act of bankruptcy, is used in both the equity and bankruptcy meanings of the term.

Moreover, receivership as an act of bankruptcy must be general, not special. For example, if a debtor's property is being 19

BANKRUPTCY 

foreclosed and a receiver is appointed in connection with the foreclosure, such receivership does not constitute an act of bankruptcy.

Sixth act of bankruptcy: written admission. If a person ad mits in writing his inability to pay his debts and his willingness to be adjudged a bankrupt, he commits an act of bankruptcy. 

The clearest admission of willingness to be adjudged a bankrupt is, of course, the filing of a voluntary petition in bankruptcy. 

However, many persons are unwilling to take the formal step of filing a petition in court, and yet are willing to admit their inability to pay their debts and their willingness to be adjudged a bankrupt.

Burden of proving insolvency. When creditors file an involuntary petition under the second, third or fifth acts of bankruptcy, they have the burden of proving insolvency. That is, the law presumes solvency unless the creditor can prove insolvency. Under the first act of bankruptcy, the debtor has the burden of establishing solvency if he wishes to urge it as a complete defense. 

That is, the law presumes insolvency unless the debtor can prove solvency. But even where the burden of proving insolvency is on the creditors, the law will shift this burden to the debtor, and will require him to prove solvency, if he fails to produce his books and records on the hearing. That is, the law in such a case will presume that the debtor is insolvent unless he can prove the contrary.

The question as to who has the burden on the subject of solvency may be important. If, where insolvency must be established as a basis for bankruptcy, the burden of proof as to insolvency is on the creditors, and no satisfactory proof of insolvency is adduced, the debtor cannot be adjudicated a bankrupt. 

On the other hand, if the burden of proving solvency is on the debtor, and no satisfactory proof is offered on the subject, the court will presume insolvency, and the debtor may be adjudicated a bankrupt.

Petition may recite one or more acts. An involuntary petition in bankruptcy is not necessarily limited to a recital of a single act of bankruptcy. As a matter of practice, it is customary for the petition to recite several acts of bankruptcy. Indeed, where a debtor has committed one act of bankruptcy, it may be found that he has committed others as well; and if this 20

is so, the petition should set forth each act assigned as a ground for bankruptcy, because sometimes one act can be readily established on the hearing, where others cannot.

Often, also, a single transaction may constitute several acts of bankruptcy; for example, a fraudulent transfer with intent to hinder, delay and defraud creditors, may also constitute a preferential transfer to a particular creditor. 

In Boston West Africa Trading Co. v. Quaker City Moroc co Co., 261 Fed. 665 (C.C.A., Mass.), the owner of all the stock of a trading corporation, within four months prior to the filing of a petition, transferred the greater part of the corporation's bank account to himself in payment of alleged claims. This bank account constituted practically all the corporation's assets, and the transfer left insufficient assets to pay other claims. The court held that such a transaction constituted not only a preference, but also a fraudulent transfer with intent to hinder, delay and defraud creditors, and the petition was sustained on both grounds. 21

CHAPTER INTERMEDIATE CUSTODY 

Status of alleged bankrupt upon filing of petition. When a voluntary petition is filed, the effect is the same as an adjudication that the petitioner is bankrupt. However, when an involuntary petition is filed, there remains the necessity for a hearing to determine the sufficiency and truth of the allegations set forth in the petition. If the allegations are insufficient in form or in law, or if they are unfounded in fact, the petition will be dismissed, and no adjudication will follow. But until a hearing is had on the petition and the answer thereto, what is the status of the alleged bankrupt and his property? May he do as he pleases with his property?

The answer to the latter question is by no means an unqualified affirmative. The situation is not quite the same as in the case of a summons or similar writ served in an ordinary law action. 

The mere service of a summons in an ordinary law action has no effect on a defendant or his property, except to notify him that he is required to answer a complaint, and that the court takes jurisdiction over him for such purpose. If the complaint is not answered, judgment may be taken against him by default. If the complaint is answered and the trial goes against him, the resulting judgment may constitute a lien against his property. But until then, and in the absence of any attachment, the defendant has complete control over his property. 

This is not true of bankruptcy. The filing of a petition in bankruptcy gives the court jurisdiction over the property of the alleged bankrupt. It is notice to the world that the court has taken over such jurisdiction for the purpose of determining the status of the alleged bankrupt and, in case of adjudication, for the purpose of settling and distributing his estate. It has the same effect as if the alleged bankrupt's property had been attached, or as if an injunction had been issued restraining all persons from interfering with his property.

Thus, upon the filing of an involuntary petition against a debtor, the status of his property undergoes a change. Instead of being within the absolute ownership, direction and control of the debtor; instead of being subject to the claims of creditors in the order in which they may sue and recover judgments, the debtor's property has now become subject to the jurisdiction of the 22

INTERMEDIATE CUSTODY 

bankruptcy court. It is now subject, in case of an adjudication, to distribution among all creditors alike in proportion to their claims and after payment of fees and expenses, or, if the petition is dismissed, it is to be returned to the absolute control and possession of the debtor. In the latter case, should the petition have been filed without just ground, the debtor has his remedy by an action for slander of credit.

Jurisdiction v. actual custody. All this does not mean that the court takes actual possession and custody of the debtor's property. It merely means that for the purpose of issuing any orders affecting such property, the court has assumed and acquired jurisdiction. Thereafter, the alleged bankrupt may continue to conduct his business and to retain actual custody and possession of his assets; but he does so as a trustee, and his title and possession will be deemed the title and possession of a trustee for the benefit of his creditors, at least until there is an adjudication or dismissal. In all dealings by the alleged bankrupt in respect to such property, he will be charged with the same degree of good faith and fair dealing that attaches to the dealings by a trustee in relation to the subject matter of his trust. This is true, even though no receiver or marshal has been actually placed in possession of the debtor's property.

Once the petition is filed, liens acquired thereafter are no longer valid, actions in replevin will no longer lie, and attachments are of no avail.

Liens acquired after petition filed. In Silverblatt v. Forcey, 11 Fed. Supp. 484, (D.C., Pa.), an involuntary petition was filed on December 1, 1933. There was no adjudication, however, until January 9, 1934. On December 18, 1933 the alleged bankrupt, who was an executor of an estate, executed and delivered to his co-executor a mortgage on his real property. On suit by the trustee appointed after adjudication, the District Court ordered the mortgage vacated and delivered up for cancellation. The Circuit Court of Appeals affirmed the decision (Forcey v. Silverblatt, 78 Fed. (2d) 1010).

The filing of a petition, however, has no effect upon preexisting liens, or upon the remedy for their enforcement. For example, if a mortgage foreclosure was commenced before the filing of a petition, it is immaterial that the foreclosure sale takes place after the filing of the petition. 

In Robinson v. Kay, 7 Fed. (2d) 576 (C.C.A., Hawaii) the holder of a first mortgage instituted foreclosure proceedings 23

BANKRUPTCY 

on November 10, 1922. An involuntary petition was filed against the mortgagor on December 6, 1922. The foreclosure sale took place on December 16, 1922. Upon this sale, a man named 

Robinson bought in the property and later resold it to a man named Woorall. The mortgagor was adjudicated a bankrupt on 

December 28, 1922, and a trustee was appointed on April 6, 1923. The trustee sued Robinson and Woorall to vacate both sales, or, in the alternative, for the difference between the amount received by Robinson on the resale and the amount of the mortgage liens and interest. The court sustained the foreclosure and dismissed the trustee's suit.

No replevin after petition filed. Recovery of possession is the essence of a suit in replevin. Since constructive possession is assumed by the bankruptcy court upon the filing of a petition, a suit in replevin will not lie after the petition is filed. 

In In re Wellmade Gas Mantle Co., 230 Fed. 502 (D.C., Mass.), affirmed by the Circuit Court in Petition of Friedlander, 233 Fed. 250, the respondent replevied certain property from the possession of the bankrupt upon a writ issued by the Municipal Court of the City of Boston. The writ was issued after the involuntary petition was filed, but before appointment of a receiver or adjudication in bankruptcy. It was claimed that the bankrupt had procured the property through fraud, and the seller therefore elected to rescind. The vendor's contention was, that since the sale was rescindable and the vendor had exercised his option to rescind by proceeding in replevin, the goods were not part of the bankrupt estate. The court, however, refused to concur in this view. It held that since an involuntary petition in bankruptcy had been filed before a judgment was procured in the replevin suit, the bankruptcy court had acquired jurisdiction to determine whether or not the seller was entitled to rescind. 

Custody and jurisdiction over all the bankrupt's assets had passed to the bankrupt immediately upon the filing of the petition. 

Ultimately, following adjudication, this custody passed to the trustee. True, the bankrupt's title might be challenged because of his fraud, and the sale might be subject to rescission; but the determination of this issue was for the bankruptcy court, not some outside jurisdiction.

Provisional remedies. Human nature being what it is, the constructive jurisdiction assumed by a bankruptcy court immediately upon the filing of a petition is frequently not to a debtor's liking. He becomes exposed to the temptation of continuing to 24

INTERMEDIATE CUSTODY 

do as he pleases with what he naturally regards as his own. 

Creditors are thus exposed to the risk of coming into a depleted estate when an adjudication is had and a trustee is appointed. 

For the bankrupt may sell his property. He may remove it. 

He may conceal it. He may waste it. Third parties, with or without the bankrupt's connivance, may make away with his assets. And thus the constructive jurisdiction over a debtor's property assumed by a bankruptcy court upon the filing of a petition may have to be implemented with specific remedies which will anticipate abuses rather than seek redress after the damage. has been done. Such remedies are referred to as provisional remedies. They include: 

1. Seizure 

2. Injunction 

3. Contempt 

4. Receivership.

These remedies are by no means mutually exclusive. Any two or more of them may be employed together. For example, in one and the same proceeding, the court may order a seizure of assets; it may grant an injunction restraining interference with its custody of the debtor's property; it may punish a violator for contempt of court; and it may appoint a receiver to take over actual custody pending adjudication or dismissal.

Seizure. Upon a proper showing of the necessity for such action, the court may direct a seizure of any property and assets of the alleged bankrupt, in order to safeguard the interests of the bankrupt estate. If there is no receiver, a marshal will seize the property upon order of the court.

The distinction between a summary proceeding and a plenary suit has been discussed in a previous chapter. Where the property is in the possession of the bankrupt, the right to seize it summarily in case of necessity is beyond question. However, if the property is adversely claimed by a third party, and is in the actual possession of such adverse claimant, the court will not direct a summary seizure, but will give the third party an opportunity to be heard before a seizure will be directed. The proper practice in such cases is for the trustee subsequently appointed to bring a plenary suit for the recovery of the property. However, if the adverse claim by a third party is obviously sham and insubstantial, the court may direct a summary seizure.

A warrant of seizure is like a warrant of attachment. It is 25

BANKRUPTCY 

a writ authorizing the officer to whom it is directed to seize property and bring it into the actual custody of the law. Like a warrant of attachment, a warrant of seizure must be supported by an affidavit showing the need for such a drastic remedy; and a bond must be given to protect the bankrupt, or the creditor from whose possession the property is taken, in case the seizure turns out to have been wrongful.

A warrant of seizure is usually signed by the judge, but if a judge is absent, the referee may sign it. 

The right of seizure is particularly important where there is no receiver. An application for a warrant of seizure may be made by any creditor or party in interest. 

Injunction. A court may order an injunction to restrain any unlawful interference with the possession and custody of a bankrupt's property at any stage of the bankruptcy proceeding. This may be particularly important during the intermediate period between the filing of a petition and the appointment of a trustee. 

In In re Kleinhans, 113 Fed. 107 (D.C., N.Y.) the bankruptcy court restrained a landlord from prosecuting summary proceedings in the state court to oust the receiver from occupancy of store premises formerly occupied by the bankrupt. 

In In re Lombardy Inn Co., 266 Fed. 394 (D.C., Mass.) a landlord was enjoined from prosecuting a pending ejectment suit against the bankrupt until the receiver had reasonable time to decide what to do.

Contempt. The remedy of contempt is sometimes necessary to enforce respect for and compliance with the orders of a bankruptcy court. As an illustration, we may take the case of In re 

Karky, 44 Fed. (2d) 417 (D.C., Wis.). In this case the bankrupts were tenants of certain premises in which they conducted a cafe. 

Forty-five minutes before they were adjudicated bankrupts, certain persons entitled to possession of the premises, instead of applying to the bankruptcy court for permission to gain possession, forcibly took possession themselves and retained such possession "to the exclusion of the bankruptcy court and its officials, exercising dominion over the same, and, though aware that that court had the exclusive right to the custody and possession of the personal property therein, partially affixed to the realty, instead of applying to the court for permission to remove the same from the premises, deliberately assumed, 26

INTERMEDIATE CUSTODY 

over the objection of the receiver, full right to tear the fixtures and booths from the realty and to remove the same to a storage place of their own selection. Whether such action was wise, whether the place selected was a proper and secure one, are beside the real question. The court alone had the right to determine those questions, and when the respondents ignored the court's jurisdiction and autocratically assumed power to determine the course of procedure which properly could be determined only by the court, they exhibited an attitude contumacious in character, which the court cannot safely ignore. Otherwise, the precedent might lead to such steps as to destroy the exercise of any jurisdiction by the court and to set at naught the orders thereof."

The court imposed a fine of $200 and ordered the offenders committed to the county jail until the fine was paid. 

Contempt for failure to obey turnover order. Failure to obey a court order to turn over books, documents, records or assets of the bankrupt is punishable as a contempt. 

In In re J. H. Jackson Co., Inc., 18 Fed. (2d) 688 (D.C., N.Y.) the receiver in bankruptcy applied for an order directing the president of the bankrupt concern to deliver the books of the company to the receiver. In opposing such application the president stated in his affidavits that "through forgetfulness," the books were left by him in a cab and lost. This excuse was not persuasive with the court, which characterized the statements set forth by the president in his affidavit as unworthy of belief, and ordered the books turned over. The president appealed, the order was affirmed, and still the books were not produced. Thereupon the receiver moved to punish the bankrupt's president for contempt. 

The proceedings are set forth in the opinion of the court: "In opposition to this motion to punish him, the respondent interposes an affidavit. It does not state that there has been any change in the possession of the books since the granting of the original order. It states only inability to comply with the order by reason of the facts set forth in the affidavit in opposition to the original order, which affidavit he incorporates in this proceeding. The order directing him to produce the books is at least prima facie evidence of bankrupt's possession of the books. The answering affidavits set forth no allegations of fact other than such as were adjudged to be untrue and unworthy of belief and sham. The answer- 27

BANKRUPTCY 

ing affidavits, therefore, present no issue to be tried. The motion, therefore, must be granted to enforce compliance with the order."

Receivership: who may apply for. The right to apply for receivership is not limited to any one or more of the petitioning creditors. Any creditor having a provable claim may apply for receivership. Hill Co. v. U. S. Fidelity Co., 265 Ill. 534, 107 

N.E. 194, 33 A.B.R. 781. The bankrupt himself, if necessary, may apply for receivership.

Formerly the Bankruptcy Act provided that a receiver would be appointed only when absolutely necessary for the preservation of the estate. The word "absolutely" was eliminated from the statute by the 1938 amendments. 

Receiver's functions.

The functions of a receiver in bankruptcy have been defined as "preservative" rather than "administrative." A receiver may not continue the business of a bankrupt unless authorized by the court to do so. He may not sell any of the assets unless they are perishable and such sale is necessary to preserve the bankrupt estate, or unless he has received authority from the court to continue the business of the bankrupt. 

Ancillary receiver. A bankruptcy court not only has jurisdiction over the assets of a bankrupt in the district of his domicile, but it may appoint an ancillary receiver to take possession and custody of a bankrupt's assets in any outside jurisdiction, where such appointment is necessary to preserve such assets. 

Right of receiver to maintain plenary suit. It was formerly held doubtful whether a receiver could maintain a plenary suit. 

Under the Act of 1938, however, the court may now authorize a receiver to commence, prosecute or defend any suit on behalf of the bankrupt estate in any jurisdiction, until either the petition is dismissed or a trustee is duly qualified. 28

CHAPTER RIGHTS, DUTIES AND LIABILITIES OF BANKRUPT 

Right to counsel. Whether proceedings be voluntary or involuntary, a bankrupt is entitled to counsel so that he may be duly advised of his legal rights. However, the court may re-examine any fee which the bankrupt pays his attorney in contemplation of bankruptcy, and may require any amount deemed in excess of a reasonable fee, to be turned over to the estate as an unlawful preference.

Exemptions of bankrupts generally. State laws throughout the country prescribe various personal exemptions immune from legal process, such as wearing apparel, wages, jewelry, etc., and various forms of occupational exemptions, such as growing crops, tools and implements of trade. What may constitute an exemption for one person will not necessarily constitute an exemption for another. For example, pianos as part of a stock in trade would not ordinarily be exempt; but a piano used principally in the teaching of music and constituting a part of the household furniture has been held to be exempt. Hill v. Binford, 91 S.W. (2d) 488 (Tex. Civ. App.). An ordinary ring, such as a wedding ring, might be exempt, but a diamond ring has been declared not to be exempt. Rivas v. Noble, 241 Fed. 673, 154 C.C.A. 431 (Fla.) ; 

Langever v. Stitt, 237 Fed. 83, 150 C.C.A. (Tex.) 285. 

Insurance exemptions; in general. The rights of bankrupts in connection with insurance policies depend upon whether the policy covers property or life. 

The rule governing property insurance is fairly uniform: a bankrupt's property insurance forms part of his estate, and the bankrupt is entitled to no exemption in respect to such insurance. 

In regard to life insurance, however, the rule is by no means uniform. To determine the bankrupt's rights in respect to life insurance, it is necessary to consider whether the bankrupt is himself the policy holder, thus having an interest in the cash surrender value of the policy, or whether he is the beneficiary under a policy insuring the life of another. Even when this distinction is made, it will be found that the rule varies in the different states, and the question of a bankrupt's exemptions is governed by the law of the state where the bankrupt is domiciled. 

Insurance exemptions: cash surrender values. In the absence of a statutory exemption, the general rule is that a life insurance 29

BANKRUPTCY 

policy which has a present cash surrender value is an asset of the estate, but the bankrupt has a right to pay the cash surrender value to the trustee in lieu of turning over the policy. This rule has been modified in some states by statutes governing insurance exemptions, so that in order to determine whether the cash surrender value of a life insurance policy is or is not an asset of the estate, it is necessary to determine whether in a particular state a statute has been adopted exempting the proceeds of life insurance policies from liability for the debts of the insured. In some states, the statute exempts the cash surrender value if the bankrupt insured has designated a third party beneficiary without reserving the right to change the beneficiary, but does not exempt the cash surrender value if the insured has reserved the right to change the beneficiary. 

This is on the theory that in the former case, the bankrupt has no control over the proceeds, whereas in the latter case he has. In other states, however, for example, New York, the cash surrender value of a policy taken out by the bankrupt in favor of a third party beneficiary is exempt whether the bankrupt has reserved the right to change the beneficiary or not. 

Insurance exemptions: right to proceeds where bankrupt is beneficiary. Where the bankrupt is a beneficiary under a policy insuring the life of another, he has no right to keep the policy intact by merely paying over the cash surrender value to the trustee, because such cash surrender value belongs to the policy holder, not to the bankrupt beneficiary. In the absence of a statutory exemption, the rule in such cases is, that if the policy holder has not reserved the right to change the beneficiary, the bankrupt beneficiary's rights are vested and the policy goes to the trustee; but if the policy holder has reserved the right to change the beneficiary, the bankrupt beneficiary's rights are not vested, and the policy does not go to the trustee. This rule, however, may be modified by statute.

State statutory exemptions governing insurance. Because of the importance of rights and exemptions in connection with insurance policies affecting a bankrupt estate, the New York statute in this connection may be of note. Its provisions may be summarized as follows: 

(1) Policy taken out by bankrupt on his own life for benefit of third party. As bearing on a policy taken out by a person on his own life for the benefit of a third party, followed by bankruptcy of the policy holder, the New York statute provides: 30

RIGHTS, DUTIES AND LIABILITIES OF BANKRUPT 

"If any policy of insurance has been or shall be effected by any person on his own life in favor of a third person beneficiary, or made payable, by assignment, change of beneficiary or otherwise, to a third person, such third person beneficiary, assignee or payee shall be entitled to the proceeds and avails of such policy as against the creditors, personal representatives, trustees in bankruptcy and receivers in state and federal courts of the person effecting the insurance." (2) Policy taken out on life of another, for one's own benefit, where person insured becomes bankrupt. As bearing on a policy taken out by a person for the benefit of himself but on the life of another person, followed by the latter's bankruptcy, the statute provides: "If any policy of insurance has been or shall be effected by any person upon the life of another person in favor of the person effecting the same or made payable, by assignment, change of beneficiary or otherwise, to such person, the latter shall be entitled to the proceeds and avails of such policy as against the creditors, personal representatives, trustees in bankruptcy and receivers in state and federal courts of the person insured;" (3) Policy taken out by wife for her own benefit, on life of bankrupt husband. And in further reference to "(2)" above, if the person who takes out the policy is the wife of the bankrupt whose life is insured, the statute provides: "if the person effecting such insurance shall be the wife of the insured, she shall be entitled to the proceeds and avails of such policy as against her own creditors, trustees in bankruptcy and receivers in state and federal courts." (4) Policy taken out by one person on life of another for benefit of third party, where either of first two parties becomes bankrupt. As bearing on a policy taken out by one person on the life of another for the benefit of a third party, where either of the first two parties becomes bankrupt, the statute provides: "If any policy of insurance has been or shall be effected by any person on the life of another person in favor of a third person beneficiary, or made payable, by assignment, change of beneficiary or otherwise, to a third per- 31

BANKRUPTCY 

son, such third person beneficiary, assignee or payee shall be entitled to the proceeds and avails of such policy as against the creditors, personal representatives, trustees in bankruptcy and receivers in state and federal courts of the person insured and of the person effecting the insurance." (5) Insurance benefits exempted. As to the insurance benefits included in the exemptions, the statute provides: "The term 'proceeds and avails' shall include death benefits, cash surrender and loan value, premiums waived, and dividends, whether used in reduction of the premiums or in whatsoever manner used or applied, excepting only where the debtor has, subsequent to the issuance of the policy, actually elected to receive the dividends in cash." (6) Right to change beneficiary immaterial. The statute makes it immaterial whether or not the insured reserves the right to change the beneficiary, or reserves the benefits to himself if the beneficiary predeceases him: "The provisions of this section shall be applicable whether or not the right is reserved in any such policy to change the beneficiary therein designated, and whether or not the policy is made payable to the person whose life is insured if the beneficiary, assignee or payee shall predecease such person; and no person shall be compelled to exercise any rights, powers, options or privileges under such policy."

Purpose of insurance exemptions. The primary purpose of state insurance exemptions is not to protect the bankrupt from his creditors, but to protect the beneficiaries from the bankrupt's creditors. As the court said in In re Messinger, 29 Fed. (2d) 158 (C.C.A., N.Y.), referring to the New York statute: "The statute does not exempt the bankrupt if he exercises his reserved power to change the beneficiary for his personal advantage, and, indeed, precludes an exemption in such case by saying that the 'beneficiary *** other than the insured' shall be entitled to the proceeds and avails. But it plainly does attempt to exempt the 'proceeds and avails,' so far as beneficiaries, other than the bankrupt, may have an interest in the policy. It does not protect the insured against his creditors, and only seeks to prevent them from affecting the rights of the beneficiaries other than himself.” 32

RIGHTS, DUTIES AND LIABILITIES OF BANKRUPT 

Duties of bankrupt: in general. The primary duty of a bankrupt is to surrender all his property of whatsoever nature, except property exempt by law. The Act charges the bankrupt with specific duties, including attendance at meetings, obedience to court orders, verification of proofs of claim and reporting irregularities therein to the trustee, the execution and delivery of all necessary papers, the preparation of schedules, the submission of a preliminary statement of his affairs, the submission of himself to examination, and the filing of cost inventories when required by the court.

Attendance at creditors' meetings. The bankrupt must attend the first meeting of creditors, the hearing upon objections to his discharge, if any, and at such other times as the court may order. 

Attendance at the first meeting of creditors is particularly important, so that the bankrupt may assist in checking creditors' claims and help to determine, in case of dispute, which creditors are qualified to vote and which are not. The bankrupt must attend both the first meeting of creditors and the hearing on objections, if any, to his discharge, without the necessity of any order being served upon him for this purpose. In all other cases, the bankrupt is not required to attend meetings unless ordered by the court to do so. 

If a bankrupt is about to leave the jurisdiction in order to avoid attending the first meeting, a warrant may issue for his arrest to insure his attendance at the meeting. 

Obedience to court orders. The bankrupt must comply strictly with all orders of the court. For a failure to do so, he may be arrested and punished for contempt, either by fine, imprisonment or both. In addition, such non-compliance will bar his discharge in bankruptcy.

Verifying proofs of claim and reporting irregularities. The bankrupt must examine and report to the trustee concerning the correctness of all proofs of claim filed against his estate. He must immediately inform his trustee of any attempt which comes to his knowledge, by creditors or other persons, to evade the provisions of the Bankruptcy Act. If the bankrupt learns that any person has proved a false claim against the estate, he must make immediate disclosure of this fact to the trustee. 

Execution and delivery of papers. The bankrupt must execute and deliver such papers as may be ordered by the court, including the execution and delivery to the trustee of any documents necessary to transfer property in foreign countries. 33

BANKRUPTCY 

Preparation and filing of schedules. The bankrupt must prepare in triplicate, make oath to, and file, within five days after adjudication if he is an involuntary bankrupt, and with his petition if he is a voluntary bankrupt, a schedule of his property showing amount, kind, location, and value of each item, and a list of all creditors-whether their claims be liquidated or unliquidated, fixed or contingent, disputed or admitted-showing their names, residences, amounts due and similar details. 

Preliminary statement of affairs: advance information before first meeting. Prior to 1938 attorneys for creditors desiring to examine a bankrupt at the first meeting of creditors were handicapped by absence of advance information. Examinations were necessarily "fishing expeditions" for possible irregularities, clues to hidden assets, etc. One of the Chandler Act amendments of 

1938 made it obligatory upon bankrupts to furnish a preliminary statement of affairs in advance of the first meeting, as an intelligent basis upon which to examine the bankrupt. This statement must be filed in triplicate with the court at least five days prior to the first meeting of creditors, on a form prescribed by the Supreme Court. One form governs bankrupts or debtors who are not engaged in business, and another those who are. 

Bankrupts or debtors not engaged in business must furnish the following information: 

Name and address; 

Occupation and income; 

Income tax returns, where and for what years filed;

Bank accounts and safe deposit boxes; 

Books and records; 

Property held in trust; 

Prior bankruptcy or other proceedings;

Assignments for the benefit of creditors;
