Suits, executions and attachments against the bankrupt or debtor: 

Loans repaid; 

Transfers of property; and Losses.

Bankrupts or debtors engaged in business, in addition to the information listed above, must furnish financial statements, inventories, income other than from the operation of the business, and accounts receivable. 34

RIGHTS, DUTIES AND LIABILITIES OF BANKRUPT 

Partnerships and corporations must furnish complete information as to personal withdrawals, including loans to partners, officers, directors or managing executives, as well as the names of the members of the firm and of officers, directors, managers and the principal stockholders of a corporation.

The preliminary statement of affairs must be sworn to by the bankrupt or debtor, who must be very careful to make sure that the statements are correct in every detail. If any part of such preliminary statement be false in any respect, or if there has been any concealment of assets, the bankrupt is not only subject to the punishment described in a subsequent chapter on bankruptcy offenses, but he will be unable to secure his discharge in bankruptcy. 

In In re Lovich, 34 Fed. Supp. 85 (D.C., N.Y., June 28, 1940) a firm consisting of two persons was duly adjudicated bankrupt. The partners in their statement of affairs answered one of the questions to the effect that no financial statement had been given by them. This answer was false. Upon the hearing of objections to a discharge, the partners urged that the statement had been prepared by their manager, and, although they had signed it, they were personally ignorant of the misstatement. The referee, however, rejected the excuse, and upon appeal, the court sustained the referee and cited the referee's reasons in refusing to grant a discharge: "I think that the statement of affairs is to be regarded as a very solemn and a very important part of a bankruptcy proceeding, as to the execution and after use thereof. If these bankrupts are to be excused from responsibility for the statement that there were no financial statements given, when there were, why we might just as well dispense with the statement of affairs as an unnecessary and superfluous part of bankruptcy administration; therefore, without imputing any intent to deceive creditors or any fraudulent purpose on their part. I feel obliged to find that this objection is sustained." 

Examinations. At the first meeting of creditors, at the hearing upon objections to his discharge, if any, and at such other times as the court may order, the bankrupt must submit to an examination concerning the conduct of his business, the cause of his bankruptcy, his dealings with creditors and others, the amount, kind and whereabouts of his property, and all matters which may affect the administration and settlement of his estate or the granting of his discharge. Formerly, a bankrupt might refuse to answer questions on the constitutional ground that such testimony might tend to "incriminate or degrade" him. A bankrupt may 35

BANKRUPTCY 

no longer refuse to testify on constitutional grounds. The Bankruptcy Act, as amended in 1938, provides that a bankrupt's testimony shall not be offered against him in any criminal proceeding, except testimony given by him in seeking a discharge, which, being voluntary, and for the bankrupt's own benefit, is not enitled to the constitutional exemption.

Inventory of costs. The court may require a bankrupt to file an inventory of costs as of the date of bankruptcy, as a basis for checking any claim that goods were sold at a loss. In the absence of a cost inventory, where ordered, the court presumes that the bankrupt's goods were not sold at a loss. Likewise, if the bankrupt fails to produce books and records showing cost of goods sold, he has the burden of proving that sales were made at a loss. 

Liability to arrest. A bankrupt is not only liable to arrest and imprisonment for the commission of one of the bankruptcy offenses, but of course he is subject to imprisonment for the commission of any other crime under the laws of any state. Bankruptcy proceedings can in no way be allowed to impede the prosecution of persons for crimes such as larceny, embezzlement, arson, etc., except that where a person is imprisoned for the commission of such crimes, he may be temporarily taken out of jail for the purpose of attending bankruptcy hearings requiring his presence. 

Where it is claimed that a bankrupt has been imprisoned without just cause, the proper remedy is to obtain a hearing on the cause of his detention. This can be done on a writ of habeas corpus, which can be signed only by the judge. 

Death or insanity of bankrupts. The death or insanity of a bankrupt does not abate the proceedings: the estate continues to be administered and is concluded in the same manner, as far as possible, as if the bankrupt had not died or become insane. However, as previously pointed out, bankruptcy proceedings cannot be instituted against the estate of a deceased person. 36

CHAPTER ADMINISTRATION OF ESTATE 

In general. The principal burden of administering an estate in bankruptcy is upon the trustee, who is usually appointed at the instance of the creditors, whom he represents in administering the estate. The basic objective of the trustee, in administering the estate, is to gather in all possible assets of the bankrupt, and to make such assets available for distribution to creditors, after payment of fees and expenses.✓

Creditors' meetings. Not less than ten nor more than thirty days after the adjudication, the referee calls and presides over the first meeting of creditors. Subsequent meetings may be called by the referee whenever required. One-fourth or more of the creditors may request that subsequent meetings be called. At the first meeting, creditors file "proofs of claim" (more particularly described in a subsequent chapter). The referee may allow or disallow any claim.

Voting by creditors. Only creditors whose claims have been allowed may vote at creditor's meetings. Such creditors may vote personally or by a representative. Voting is governed by a majority in number as well as in amount. For example, ten creditors aggregating $20,000 in claims cannot outvote two creditors with a total of $50,000 in claims, and neither can the latter outvote the former, because neither group has a majority in number and amount. Claims of $50 or less are counted in computing the amount, but not in computing the number of creditors voting. Secured or priority creditors (to whom further reference will be made in a later chapter) may not vote unless the amounts of their claims exceed the values of their securities or priorities. 

Appointment of trustee: creditors' committees. The creditors at their first meeting may elect one or three trustees, subject to confirmation and approval by the court. If the creditors do not elect a trustee, or if the trustee elected fails to qualify, the referee appoints a trustee. The creditors may at their first meeting also designate a committee of not less than three creditors. This committee may consult and advise with the trustee, make recommendations, and submit to the court any questions affecting the administration of the estate. 37

BANKRUPTCY 

Trustee's title. The trustee, by operation of law, automatically takes over all the bankrupt's right, title and interest in any of his property, real or personal, tangible or intangible. This includes, of course, only the property of which the bankrupt himself is the owner. It does not include the property of a bankrupt's husband, wife or children. Neither does it include the property of a corporation whose entire capital stock is owned by a bankrupt, because such property belongs to the corporation; but it does include the stock of the corporation. 

Exempt property, as pointed out in the previous chapter, does not pass to the bankrupt's estate.

Duties of trustee. Primarily, the trustee's function is to collect and reduce to money, under the direction of the court and as expeditiously as possible, all property, real or personal, tangible or intangible, which may belong to the bankrupt, wherever such property may be located and whoever may be in possession of it. All other duties of the trustee are subordinate and incidental to this one. Specifically, the duties of trustees in bankruptcy are set forth in section 47 of the Bankruptcy Act, which requires that trustees shall- (1) Collect and reduce to money the property of the estates for which they are trustees, under the direction of the court, and close up the estates as expeditiously as is compatible with the best interests of the parties. in interest; (2) Deposit all money received by them in designated depositories; (3) Account for and pay over to the estates under their control all interest received by them upon funds belonging to such estates: (4) Disburse money only by check or draft on such depositories; (5) Keep records and accounts showing all amounts and items of property received and from what sources, all amounts expended and for what purposes, and all items of property disposed of; (6) Set apart the bankrupts' exemptions allowed by law, if claimed, and report the items and estimated value thereof to the courts as soon as practicable after their appointment; 38

ADMINISTRATION OF ESTATE 

(7) Examine the bankrupts (8) (a) (b) at the first meeting of creditors or at other meetings specially fixed for that purpose, unless they shall already have been fully examined by the referees, receivers, or creditors, and upon the hearing of objections, if any, to their discharges, unless otherwise ordered by the court;

Examine all proofs of claim and object to the allowance of such claims as may be improper; (9) Oppose, at the expense of estates, the discharges of bankrupts when they deem it advisable to do so; 

Furnish such information concerning the estates of which they are trustees and their administration as may be requested by parties in interest; (10) (11) Pay dividends within ten days after they are declared by the referees; (12) (13) Report to the courts in writing the condition of the estates, the amounts of money on hand, and such other details as may be required by the courts, within the first month after their appointment and every two months thereafter, unless otherwise ordered by the courts;

Make final reports and file final accounts with the courts fifteen days before the days fixed for the final meetings of the creditors; and (14) Lay before the final meetings of the creditors detailed statements of the administration of the estates. 

Trustee's duty to examine bankrupt. The trustee must examine the bankrupt at the first meeting of creditors, or at other meetings especially fixed for that purpose, unless the bankrupt has already been examined by the referee, receiver or creditors. 

The trustee must satisfy himself that the bankrupt has withheld no property which should go into the estate, and that he has not been guilty of a voidable preference, transfer or lien in favor of some particular creditor or creditors. 

Trustee's duty to set aside voidable preferences, transfers and liens. It is a trustee's duty to take the necessary steps to set aside any unlawful preference, transfer or lien. A solvent person may 39

BANKRUPTCY 

Voilable prefer any creditor he pleases; but an insolvent person cannot prefer one creditor over another without causing injury to the latter. Similarly, when an insolvent person transfers any of his property for an inadequate consideration, or permits a lien to be placed on his property for the benefit of a favored creditor, he thereby depreciates the value of the estate available to creditors. generally. 

What constitutes voidable preference. A preference is not voidable unless the following elements are present: (1) a transfer; (2) insolvency at time of transfer; (3) transfer made within four months before filing of petition, or after filing and before adjudication; (4) transfer enables creditor to obtain payment of a greater percentage of his debt than other creditors of the same class; and (5) transferee had reason to believe that the transfer would effect a preference. All these elements must be present, or the preference is not voidable. 

Suppose, for example, that an insolvent father transfers stock to his son just prior to bankruptcy, and the son deposits the stock as collateral security for a loan at the bank. We thus have (1) a transfer, (2) insolvency at time of transfer, (3) transfer made within four months before filing of petition, and (4) creditor enabled to obtain payment of a greater percentage of his debt than other creditors of the same class. And yet the trustee in bankruptcy may not recover such stock as an unlawful preference if the bank had no reason to know that acceptance of the collateral would constitute a preference.

Mere suspicion by the transferee that the debtor is insolvent, is not sufficient. "It is not enough," says the United States 

Supreme Court in Grant v. Nat'l Bank, 97 U.S. 80, “that a creditor has some cause to suspect the insolvency of his debtor; but he must have such knowledge of facts as to induce a reasonable belief of his debtor's insolvency in order to invalidate a security taken for his debt." In the absence of such circumstances, "obtaining additional security or receiving payment of a debt," as the Supreme Court observes in the Grant case, "is not prohibited by law."

The Bankruptcy Act, as amended in 1938, provides (sec. 

60, subd. b) that "Any such preference may be avoided by the trustee if the creditor receiving it or to be benefited thereby or his agent acting with reference thereto has, at the time when the transfer is made, reasonable cause to believe that the debtor is insolvent." 40

ADMINISTRATION OF ESTATE 

In Batchelder v. Home National Bank, 210 Mass. 420, 105

N.E. 1052, suit was brought to set aside a preferential transfer under the following conditions: one O'Neill, a bankrupt, had for many years been borrowing money at defendant bank. Shortly prior to his bankruptcy, he had suffered a total fire loss. The cashier of the bank had persuaded him to have the insurance moneys paid over to the bank in satisfaction of unmatured notes. 

This was done. In due course, the trustee sued to set aside the preference, and obtained judgment against the bank, which appealed on the ground that there had been no sufficient proof that the bank knew of O'Neill's insolvency at the time of the transfer, and therefore that the jury had no right to make such a finding. The appellate court ruled, however, that this was a question of fact which the jury had a right to decide for itself. 

On the question of law the court observed that "To hold the defendant there must have been a reasonable cause of belief, not a mere ground of suspicion."

Voidable preferences v. fraudulent transfers. Although fraudulent transfers may also constitute voidable preferences, not all voidable preferences, as we have seen, are necessarily fraudulent transfers. Both debtor and creditor may engage in a voidable transfer with the best of intentions, but this will not make the transfer any the less voidable, if the transferee knew that the transferror was insolvent. The Bankruptcy Act recognizes the distinction, because it not only defines voidable transfers, but it also specifies the circumstances under which liens and fraudulent transfers may be set aside.

Voidable liens. The Act (sec. 67) defines a voidable lien as one obtained by attachment, judgment, levy, or other process within four months before the filing of a petition where (a) at the time the lien was obtained, the debtor was insolvent, or (b) the lien was sought or permitted in fraud of the Act. 

The court is given summary jurisdiction to determine whether a given lien is voidable, thereby rendering a plenary suit unnecessary. 

When liens valid notwithstanding insolvency. Statutory liens for taxes and debts due the government, and liens in favor of employees, contractors, mechanics, landlords, etc., may be valid even though created or perfected while the debtor was insolvent and within four months prior to the filing of the petition. 41

BANKRUPTCY 

Acts constituting fraudulent transfers. In defining fraudulent transfers, the Act provides: (1) Every transfer made and every obligation incurred by a debtor within one year prior to the filing of a petition, is fraudulent (a) As to existing creditors, if made or incurred without fair consideration by a debtor who is or will be thereby rendered insolvent, regardless of the debtor's intent; (b) (c) (d) As to existing and subsequent creditors, if made or incurred without fair consideration by a debtor who is about to engage or continue in a business for which the property left in his hands is an unreasonably small capital, regardless of the debtor's intent;

As to existing and subsequent creditors, if made or incurred without fair consideration by a debtor who intends to incur or believes he will incur debts beyond his ability to pay when they mature; and 

As to existing and subsequent creditors, if made or incurred with actual intent to hinder, delay, or defraud existing or future creditors. (2) Every transfer made or obligation incurred by a debtor within four months prior to the filing of a petition is fraudulent as to existing and subsequent creditors, if made or incurred with intent to use the consideration obtained for the transfer or obligation for the purpose of effecting a voidable preference. (3) Regardless of intent, every transfer of partnership property, and every partnership obligation incurred, within one year prior to the filing of a petition, by or against the firm, is fraudulent as to existing creditors, if the transfer was made or the obligation incurred while the partnership was insolvent, or if its effect would be to render the partnership insolvent. If the transfer was made to a partner, it is immaterial whether or not such partner promised to pay partnership debts. If the transfer was made to an outsider, it must have been without fair consideration; to be rendered fraudulent.

In all the above cases, the words, "prior to the filing of a petition" means prior to the filing of a petition in bankruptcy, or prior to the filing of a petition for corporate reorganization, or for 42

ADMINISTRATION OF ESTATE 

an arrangement. (Corporate reorganizations and arrangements will be discussed in subsequent chapters.) 

Duty to assume or reject contracts. As we shall point out in connection with the proof, allowance and payment of claims (Chapter 8), the trustee, under section 70 of the Bankruptcy Act, is allowed sixty days after adjudication within which to assume or reject any executory contract to which the bankrupt is a party. Of course, if the contract is executed so far as the other side is concerned, that is, if the bankrupt has already received the benefit of the contract, the trustee is in no position to exercise an election to assume or reject: so far as the creditor is concerned, his part of the bargain is performed, and there remains an executory obligation by the bankrupt, which is binding as a valid claim against the estate.

The trustee's assumption of a bankrupt's contract necessarily carries with it an assumption of all its obligations. He cannot assume the benefits of a contract without also assuming its burdens. For example, in Mankins v. Forward Movement Syndicate, 28 Cal. App. 285, 152 P. 313, the bankrupt had contracted to cut and remove certain timber purchased by him. This he failed to do. Subsequently, after he was adjudicated a bankrupt, the trustee assumed the contract. The owner of the timber refused to deliver it except upon payment of damages for breach of contract, in addition to payment of the price, and the court sustained this position, since the trustee, upon assuming the benefits of the contract, necessarily assumed its burdens. Had the trustee not assumed the contract, the owner of the timber would have had to file his claim and take his chances with the other general creditors. 

Actions by or against trustee. A trustee in bankruptcy may bring any action in any court in behalf of the bankrupt estate and for the benefit of creditors, and may conduct such suit, continue it or discontinue it, as his own discretion may dictate in the interests of the creditors. For this purpose he may retain an attorney or attorneys to represent him. Formerly, an attorney could not represent a trustee if he also represented a general creditor. This is no longer the law. Trustees may not only bring suit in outside courts, but suit may be brought against them to enforce any claims or rights other than those ordinarily provable by filing a proof of claim in bankruptcy. 43

BANKRUPTCY 

Trustee's duty to account. Within the first month after his appointment, and every two months thereafter, the trustee must render an accounting to the court concerning his administration of the estate. He must make a final report and file his final account, fifteen days before the day fixed for the final meeting of creditors; and he must lay before such final meeting a detailed statement of the administration of the estate. 

Objections; surcharge; approval; discharge. Any party in interest may object to the trustee's account upon a hearing set for such purpose. The referee sustains such objections if they are meritorious, and overrules them if they are not. So long as the bankrupt estate has not been closed and the trustee has not been discharged, his accounts are subject to inspection and audit by the court. If, by some wrongful act or omission of the trustee, the estate has suffered loss, the trustee may be surcharged, or held personally liable for such loss. If a trustee's account is not objected to, and the referee finds it in order, the account will be approved and the trustee discharged. The estate is thereupon closed. 44

CHAPTER  

Proofs of claim: nature, form and contents. A proof of claim in bankruptcy is a statement under oath signed by a creditor seting forth the facts in reference to his claim. These facts include, as a rule, the consideration for the claim; whether the claim is secured, and if so, how; whether any payments have been made on the claim, and if so, what; together with an affirmation that the sum claimed is justly owing from the bankrupt to the creditor. If a claim is founded on a written instrument, the original instrument must be attached.

Time for filing proofs of claims. Proofs of claims must be filed with the court or referee within six months after the first date set for the first meeting of creditors. As previously noted, however, if a creditor wants to vote at the first meeting, he must first file his proof of claim. If a creditor fails to file his proof of claim within the time allowed by law, his claim is lost. It matters. not that the claimant had no notice or knowledge of the bankruptcy proceedings until after the time had expired. First 

Nat. Bank v. Virginia Oil & Refining Co., 86 Fed. (2d) 770 (C. C. A., Tex.); certiorari denied, 57 S. Ct. 669. Neither does it matter that the claim was lost in the mail: if the time has expired before the loss is discovered, the law allows no extension of time. In re R. B. Rose Co., 43 Fed. (2d) 446 (D. C., N. Y.); 

In re Harris Bros., 5 Fed. Supp. 191 (D. C., Ark.). Even where the claim was sent to attorneys who neglected to file it, but who informed the claimant that it had been filed, the court was held to have no power to order the claim filed after the expiration period. Tarbell v. Crex Carpet Co., 90 Fed. (2d) 683 (C. C. A., Minn.).

Provable claims. Not every claim is provable in bankruptcy. 

Only those claims which are specifically enumerated in the Bankruptcy Act (sec. 63) may be proved, namely, the following: (1) Fixed liabilities, as evidenced by a judgment or a written instrument absolutely owing at the time of the filing of the petition, whether then payable or not, with interest as of the filing date, or rebate of interest if interest was prepaid. 45

BANKRUPTCY 

(2) (3) (4) (5) (6) (7) (8) Provable debts reduced to judgment after the filing of the petition and before the bankrupt's application for discharge comes up for consideration. 

Liabilities on open account, or on contract, express or implied. 

Contingent debts and contingent contractual liabilities. 

Right to recover damages in any action for negligence commenced prior to, and pending at the time of the filing of the petition.

Workmen's compensation awards against bankrupt for injuries occurring prior to adjudication.

A claim for court costs incurred in good faith by a creditor before the filing of the petition in an action to recover a provable debt.

Costs taxable against a bankrupt who at the time of the filing of the petition was plaintiff in a cause of action which the trustee declined to prosecute after notice. (9) Claim for anticipatory breach of contracts, including unexpired leases of real or personal property. 

Some of these claims call for additional comment.

Unmatured claims. If a debt based on a written instrument is absolutely owing at the time the petition is filed, it does not matter that the debt is not yet due. For example, a claim based on a promissory note is provable though the note has not yet matured.

Bank deposits: when bank may apply, against unmatured notes. 

Suppose that immediately prior to the filing of a petition, a bankrupt has a bank balance of $1500, against which he is indebted to the bank in the sum of $2000 on his unmatured note which he discounted with the bank. Suppose, further, that the bankrupt, in contemplation of filing a petition, gave the bank his check for the full amount of his deposit. Would the trustee in bankruptcy succeed in setting aside the transaction as an unlawful preference, or would the bank be allowed to apply the proceeds of the check against its $2000 claim? A brief analysis will make it evident that the first of these questions must be answered in the negative, the second in the affirmative. 46

The relationship between a bank and its depositors is that of debtor and creditor. The bank owed the bankrupt depositor $1,500, the amount of the deposit. The depositor owed the bank $2,000 on his unmatured note. Under section 68 of the Bankruptcy Act, the bank would be allowed to offset, against its $1,500 debt to the depositor, the latter's $2,000 debt to the bank. 

The depositor's check for $1,500 would merely be a direction to the bank to do what it had a right to do anyway. It would involve no real diminution of the bankrupt's estate, and hence it could not constitute a preferential transfer. N. Y. County 

Nat'l Bank v. Massey, 192 Ū.S. 138. The fact that the depositor's debt was not due would be immaterial, because under section 63 of the Bankruptcy Act, a claim based on a written instrument absolutely owing, is provable whether due at the time of the petition or not.

Precisely this situation arose in the case of Frank v. Mercantile National Bank, 182 N.Y. 264. The court pointed out that unmatured claims against a bankrupt are provable against the estate, and cited section 68 of the Bankruptcy Act which provides: "In all cases of mutual debts or mutual credits between the estate of a bankrupt and a creditor the account shall be stated and one debt shall be set off against the other, and the balance only shall be allowed or paid."

This situation, however, is one existing solely under the Bankruptcy Act. It should not be confused with a situation where the depositor is merely an ordinary debtor of a bank. In the latter case, if the depositor's note were not due, the bank would have no right to offset the amount of the unmatured note against the balance on deposit, or to refuse to honor checks drawn against the amount of the deposit. Beckwith v. Union Bank, 9 N.Y. 

211; Samuels v. Public Nat'l. Bank, 151 Misc. 200, 270 N.Y. 

Supp. 112; Jordan v. Nat'l Shoe and Leather Bank, 74 N.Y. 467. 

Suppose, for example, that the depositor in the above case discounted his six month note with the bank for $2000, then died insolvent three months later. Since a decedent cannot be adjudicated a bankrupt, the bank would have no right to offset the amount of the unmatured note against the amount of the bank account, or refuse to honor the estate's check for the amount of the balance on deposit.

Reclamations. If a person claims specific property in the bankrupt estate as his own, he must bring "reclamation proceedings" for the recovery of such property, instead of filing a proof of claim. If he files a proof of claim, he forfeits his right to the 47

BANKRUPTCY 

specific property, and becomes relegated to the status of a creditor. 

Lowell v. Brown, 280 Fed. 193, aff'd (C.C.A., Mass.) 284 

Fed. 936 (reversed on other grounds, 265 U.S. 1).

The theory of a reclamation proceeding is, not that a creditor seeks to satisfy a claim, but that the owner of certain property which is in the custody of the estate wishes to reclaim it. It is as if some one had left his umbrella on the premises of a customer the day before the latter became a bankrupt: the owner would be entitled to reclaim his umbrella.

When a seller seeks to rescind a sale to a buyer who goes into bankruptcy, he proceeds on the theory that the merchandise sold belongs to him and not to the bankrupt estate. His remedy, therefore, would be to bring reclamation proceedings.

In In re Gold Band Curtain Co., 18 Fed. Supp. 847 (D.C., N.Y.) a buyer induced a seller to make a sale on credit, by misrepresenting the buyer's assets and liabilities so as to show a net worth of $25,000, when in fact there was a substantial deficit. 

Eighteen days later the buyer made an assignment for the benefit of creditors. This was followed about a month later by a petition in bankruptcy. The seller thereupon brought proceedings to reclaim the property from the trustee, and the court sustained his right to do so. 

Trust claims. 

Where a bankrupt holds property in trust, the beneficiary, as equitable owner, may reclaim the property or its proceeds. The beneficiary is not, strictly speaking, a creditor entitled to priority, but a claimant entitled to specific property or a specific fund.

Checks drawn by bankrupt. Where the depositor of a bank, prior to his bankruptcy, issues a check which is not presented until after his bankruptcy, the holder has no claim to any specific fund, but shares with the rest of the general creditors. A check is merely an order, drawn on a bank by a creditor (depositor), requesting the bank to pay to the order of the person named as payee, a sum certain in money on demand. Until such order is honored, either by payment, or by acceptance (certification), or by the constructive application of the funds to the credit of the payee (as by crediting the proceeds to the payee on the books of the corporation),-no equitable assignment of the fund takes place, and the payee is unable to establish a specific claim upon the amount represented by the check. On the other hand, if the check has in the meantime been honored, as by certification, or by being credited to the payee on the books of the bank, an equitable assign- 48

ment takes place, and the holder may establish a specific claim upon the fund represented by the check. 

Ordinarily, the filing

Objections to and allowance of claims. 

of a proof of claim, duly executed and in proper form, constitutes prima facie evidence of the claim; and the claim will be allowed unless an objection is filed. The allowance of a claim may be objected to by any party interested in the estate. The trustee in bankruptcy must object to the allowance of any doubtful claims; and any creditor may object. Upon the filing of objections, a hearing is held before the court, and upon the evidence adduced at such hearing, the claim is either allowed or disallowed. 

Unliquidated or contingent claims. The Bankruptcy Act provides that an unliquidated or contingent claim shall not be allowed unless it can be liquidated, or its amount estimated, "in the manner and within the time directed by the court; and such claim shall not be allowed if the court shall determine that it is not capable of liquidation or of reasonable estimation or that such liquidation or estimation would unduly delay the administration of the estate or any proceeding under this Act." 

Usurious claims. Claims tainted with usury will be disallowed, unless the bankrupt is a corporation. A trustee's position in this respect is the same as, but no better than, the bankrupt's. 

If the bankrupt is a corporation, the trustee is no more able to urge usury than the corporation would have been had there been. no bankruptcy.

Landlord and tenant claims. Bankruptcy claims arising between landlord and tenant have been a source of frequent dispute and conflicting decisions. Space will not permit an exhaustive review of the authorities. The following general principles may be stated: 

Bankruptcy of landlord: Where the landlord becomes bankrupt, his leasehold interest goes to the trustee in bankruptcy, the same as any other asset, and the tenant's rights are generally unaffected. cy: 

Bankruptcy of tenant-no provision in lease as to bankrupt- Where the tenant becomes bankrupt, and there is no provision in the lease as to bankruptcy, the tenant's right to possession under the lease passes to the trustee in bankruptcy. Even where the lease gives the landlord a right to terminate the tenancy upon assignment by the tenant, the term, "assignment," used in this connection, is held not to cover assignment by operation of law, under which the trustee in bankruptcy acquires the bankrupt tenant's rights in the lease. 49

BANKRUPTCY 

Landlord's claim for rent due and unpaid at time of tenant's bankruptcy: Where, at the time of the tenant's bankruptcy, there is a balance due and accrued for unpaid rentals, the claim for such rent is a fixed liability, and is provable as such. 

Landlord's claim for future rent under unexpired lease: The provability of a landlord's claim for future rentals under an unexpired lease depends upon the language of the lease and any forfeiture provisions thereunder. It is further limited by the Bankruptcy Act to a maximum unexpired period for which the landlord may claim.

Forfeiture provisions: Leases frequently contain forfeiture provisions to the effect that upon the tenant's insolvency, bankruptcy, receivership, assignment for the benefit of creditors or similar change in status, the lease shall automatically terminate. 

Under such a clause, the lease does automatically terminate upon the happening of the event. The trustee then acquires no rights under the lease, but on the other hand, the landlord has no claim for future rentals either, since there can be no future rentals under a non-existent lease.

However, forfeiture clauses are frequently coupled with indemnity provisions. Some of these indemnity provisions are valid, others not.

If the lease provides that in addition to forfeiture in case of bankruptcy, the landlord may forthwith recover, as damages, the aggregate of rentals for the remainder of the term, such provision is construed as a penalty, and will furnish no basis for a provable claim. Kothe v. Taylor Trust, 280 U.S. 224. 

If the provision is to the effect that in addition to forfeiture, the tenant shall remain liable for aggregate future rentals, less the present rental value of the premises, it provides, in the language of the court, "a reasonable formula for ascertaining the damages. of the landlord," does not "smack of a penalty," and is enforceable. Irving Trust Co. v. A. W. Perry, 293 U. S. 307.

Not all forfeiture clauses will automatically terminate the lease. Forfeiture clauses may provide, in effect, that in case of bankruptcy, etc., the lease shall, at the lessor's option, terminate, etc. In such cases, the lease does not terminate except upon some affirmative act by the landlord showing that he does exercise his option to terminate the lease.

Bankruptcy Act limitations upon landlord's claim for future rent under unexpired lease. Under the old Act, a landlord's claim against a bankruptcy tenant, to be provable, had to be actually due and owing at the time of bankruptcy. Future rents under 50

unexpired leases were generally held to be non-provable. On the other hand, since such claims were not provable in bankruptcy, they were not dischargeable either; and bankrupts frequently found themselves burdened with heavy lease obligations notwithstanding a discharge in bankruptcy. This situation proved especially onerous during the period following the financial crash of 1929. Debtors struggling under unexpired leases with inflated rental values found the Bankruptcy Act of little avail as a gateway to rehabilitation. A start toward relief in this direction was made by the 1933 provisions for debtor relief which defined "debt" as embracing "all claims of whatever character against the debtor or his property, including a claim for future rent, whether or not such claims would otherwise constitute provable claims under this Act." But it was not until the passage of the Chandler Act in 

1938 that full relief was afforded in situations of this character. 

Right to reject executory contracts, including unexpired leases. 

Under section 70 of the present Bankruptcy Act, the trustee, within sixty days after adjudication, must assume or reject any executory contract, including unexpired leases of real property. 

Any such contract or lease not assumed or rejected within such time is deemed rejected. Within sixty days after adjudication, the trustee must file a sworn statement showing which, if any, of the bankrupt's contracts, including leases, are executory, and which of such contracts have been rejected. Of course, the fact that a trustee elects to assume a lease is in no way binding on the landlord if the lease gives the landlord the right to declare a forfeiture upon the tenant's bankruptcy.

Claims based on unexpired leases are now provable, hence dischargeable; maximum period allowable. The present Bankruptcy 

Act makes claims based on unexpired leases provable, hence dischargeable in bankruptcy. The Act, however, places a definite limitation upon the maximum unexpired period for which a landlord may lay claim to damages. It provides [sec. 63 (a) (9)] that debts may be proved and allowed when they are founded on "claims for anticipatory breach of contracts, executory in whole or in part, including unexpired leases of real or personal property." 

The theory of an anticipatory breach is that a present act of some sort (in this case, bankruptcy) makes a future breach of contract certain; hence the breach may be deemed to have occurred now, and a present claim for damages is justified. The Act goes on to provide, however, "That the claim of a landlord for damages for injury resulting from the rejection of an unexpired lease of real estate or for damages or indemnity under a covenant contained 51

BANKRUPTCY 

in such lease shall in no event be allowed in an amount exceeding the rent reserved by the lease, without acceleration, for the year next succeeding the date of the surrender of the premises to the landlord or the date of reentry of the landlord, whichever first occurs, whether before or after bankruptcy, plus an amount equal to the unpaid rent accrued, without acceleration, up to such date." 

Landlord's right to dispossess receiver or trustee. A landlord always has the right to dispossess the tenant if the tenant refuses to pay rent, or commits an act (such as bankruptcy) which gives the landlord a right to declare a forfeiture under an appropriate provision in the lease. However, the position of a trustee or receiver in bankruptcy is not quite the same as that of the tenant prior to his bankruptcy. The trustee or receiver is not required to pay the rent stipulated in the lease, but only the reasonable value of the use and occupancy of the premises. Neither can the trustee or receiver be dispossessed within the time and under the rules applicable to ordinary tenants in an ordinary court of law. 

The receiver or trustee must be given a reasonable opportunity to organize the custody and affairs of the bankrupt; otherwise, the estate might suffer distress liquidation and needless shrinkage in value. In no event, of course, may the landlord act without a court proceeding. If he desires to declare a forfeiture he may do so in the bankruptcy court. If a receiver takes possession and the landlord does nothing, the receiver owes him nothing, because the receiver merely steps into the tenant's shoes, and is entitled to enjoy possession of the premises until disturbed. If the lessor wants to hold the receiver, he must take steps in the bankruptcy court. "The lessor might come to this court, declare on the lease and broken condition, and demand possession or a new tenure, under which the receiver would be obliged to pay for use and occupation. Until he did, the receiver, holding as successor to the lessee, was not liable at all; till then the only undertaking was the lessee's." In re Manhattan Piggly Wiggly Corp'n.: Ex parte Regnis Holding Corp'n., 296 Fed. 944 (D.C., N.Y.)

Secured, preferred, and general creditors distinguished. We have already pointed out (Chapter 4) the distinction between a secured and a preferred creditor. A secured creditor is one whose debt is protected by some form of security, held either by the creditor, or by a guarantor of the debt. A preferred creditor, literally, is one who has been given a preference. Frequently, however, the term "preferred creditor" is used to designate a creditor whose claim has priority over the claims of general creditors. A general 52

creditor is one who has no security or priority in connection with his claim.

Secured creditors. Creditors who are fully secured may, of course, resort to their security in satisfaction of their claims. If there is anything left over after the claim is satisfied, the surplus goes to the trustee in bankruptcy. The Bankruptcy Act provides (section 57, subd. e): "Claims of secured creditors and those who have priority may be temporarily allowed to enable such creditors to participate in the proceedings at creditors' meetings held prior to the determination of the value of their securities or priorities, but shall be thus temporarily allowed for such sums only as to the courts seem to be owing over and above the value of their securities or priorities." The same section further provides (subd. h): "The value of securities held by secured creditors shall be determined by converting the same into money according to the terms of the agreement pursuant to which such securities were delivered to such creditors, or by such creditors and the trustee, by agreement, arbitration, compromise or litigation, as the court may direct, and the amount of such value shall be credited upon such claims, and a dividend shall be paid only on the unpaid balance." 

Priorities. Debts are accorded priority over the claims of general creditors in the following order: (1) Actual and necessary costs and expenses of preserving the estate after the filing of the petition. These include filing fees, expenses of recovering concealed or fraudulently transferred assets, trustee's expenses, attorney's fees, etc. (2) Wage claims (not exceeding $600 to any claimant) which have been earned within three months before bankruptcy, including claims of full or part time salesmen on salary or commission. (3) Costs and expenses of creditors for successfully opposing, revoking or setting aside a plan of arrangement, or a wage earner's plan, or a bankrupt's discharge; or in securing the conviction of any person for a bankruptcy offense. (4) (5) Taxes.

Debts entitled to priority by Federal law, and rent due and owing for actual use and occupancy (accrued within three months prior to bankruptcy) to a landlord entitled to priority under the laws of a particular state. (Some states give landlords a prior lien on a tenant's property.) 53

BANKRUPTCY 

(6) Debts contracted after the granting of a discharge or the confirmation of an arrangement, where the discharge or confirmation is subsequently revoked or set aside for fraud or similar reason.

Wage and tax claims have been the subject of frequent dispute in bankruptcy proceedings. 

Wage claims. As noted above, wage claims, to be entitled to priority, must not exceed $600 to any claimant, and must have been earned within three months before bankruptcy. The 

Act defines a wage earner as one "who works for wages, salary or hire, at a rate of compensation not exceeding $1,500 per year. 

Hence, a general manager of a bankrupt corporation who files a salary claim for $1,400 is not entitled to priority as a wage earner. In re Grubbs-Wiley Grocery Co., 96 Fed. 183 (D.C.,

Mo.). Neither is a certified public accountant who audits the books of a bankrupt firm. Matter of Samek & Robel, Inc., 5 Am. 

B.R. (N.S.) 683. The court has, however, held that musicians hired by a bankrupt to play on his roof garden are "servants" entitled to priority on their wage claims for services rendered within three months of the institution of bankruptcy proceedings. In re 

Caldwell, 164 Fed. 515 (D.C., Ark.). The court in that case adopted the broad definition of a servant as one who renders personal services to an employer "otherwise than in the pursuit of an independent calling," and who, in any such service, "remains entirely under the control and direction of the employer." The court cited 20 Am. & Eng. Enc. of Law (2d Ed.) 11, at page 12, as follows: "The relation of master and servant exists where the employer has the right to select the employee, the power to remove or discharge him, or the right to direct both what work shall be done and the way and manner in which it shall be done." The court then went on to point out that "A musician, employed by the day, week, or month at regular wages, while not a 'menial servant' in any sense of the word, is still one who labors for the benefit of an employer. He is not in pursuit of an independent calling, and is subject to his master's commands, and must do as directed. The fact that his work is that of an artist does not deprive him of the benefit which the law intended to give to those working for wages for their living."

Tax claims. Taxes are a prior claim upon a bankrupt estate, and any fiduciary who pays a private debt ahead of taxes runs the risk of being personally liable for such taxes if the estate is unable to pay them. The statute provides (U.S. Rev. St. sec. 54

3467, U.S.C.A. sec. 192) that every executor, administrator, assignee or other person who pays, in whole or in part, any debt due by the person or estate for whom or for which he acts, before he satisfies and pays the debt due the United States from such person or estate, shall become answerable personally to the extent of such payments for the debt so due, or for so much of it as remains due and unsatisfied. A trustee is similarly liable for rendering an estate unable to pay state taxes. However, a trustee is not liable if he had no notice of the existence of the tax. State of Delaware v. Irving Trust Co., 92 Fed. (2d) 17 (C.C.A., N. 

Y.), aff'g. 19 Fed. Supp. 231 (D.C., N. Y.); certiorari denied, 302 U.S. 754. 

Relative rights of partnership and individual creditors. In the administration of a bankrupt partnership estate, the Bankruptcy Act recognizes the rule of marshalling assets. Firm assets must first be made available for payment of firm debts, and the partners' individual assets for the payment of their respective individual debts. If, after payment of firm debts, a surplus remains, such surplus-in which each individual partner has an undivided interest may be applied to the payment of the partners' individual debts. If, after a partner pays his personal debts out of his personal assets, a surplus remains, such surplus may be applied to the payment of firm debts, provided firm assets are insufficient for this purpose. 

Dividends. A dividend in bankruptcy represents a proportion of assets in a bankrupt estate available for distribution to general creditors. Dividends in bankruptcy differ from dividends paid by a corporation in three respects: (1) (2) (3) A corporation cannot pay a dividend except out of surplus. A trustee in bankruptcy pays dividends out of the corpus or assets in the estate. 

The declaration of a dividend by a corporation does not require court approval. The payment of a dividend by a trustee in bankruptcy requires judicial approval.

The declaration of a dividend by a corporation represents a distribution of profits. Payment of a dividend by a trustee in bankruptcy represents the return, in whole or in part, of a debt.

Dividends are declared by the referee and paid by the trustee. 

The first dividend must be declared within thirty days after the 55

BANKRUPTCY 

first date set for the first meeting of creditors, provided the amount in the estate in excess of the amount necessary to pay debts which have priority, equals 5% or more of claims which have been and probably will be allowed. The first dividend cannot exceed 50% of the surplus over the monies necessary to pay claims having priority and claims not yet allowed. A final dividend cannot be declared until three months after the first one. The dividend rate must be uniform as to all unsecured creditors.

The rights of 

Claims allowed after dividend declarations. creditors who have received dividends, or in whose favor final dividends have been declared, are not affected by the subsequent proof and allowance of claims. However, before such creditors are paid any further dividends, subsequent claimants are entitled to receive dividends equal in amount to those already received by the others.

Unclaimed monies. Dividends or other monies which remain unclaimed for sixty days after the final dividend has been paid and distributed must be paid by the trustee into the bankruptcy court. The trustee must file a list of the names and post-office addresses, as far as known, of the persons entitled to such monies and the amounts respectively due them. Dividends remaining unclaimed for one year are distributed to creditors whose claims have been allowed and not paid in full. If such claims have been paid in full, the balance is paid to the bankrupt. 56

CHAPTER  

Discharge in general. As noted in the first chapter, one of the main purposes of the Bankruptcy Act is to give honest but insolvent debtors an opportunity to make a new start in life. The provisions of the Act dealing with a bankrupt's discharge are designed to wipe his slate of obligations clean, provided he has complied with the law in every respect. Hence a bankrupt is entitled to a discharge as a matter of right, on complying with the conditions of the Act; with this further proviso, that certain types of obligations are not dischargeable in bankruptcy. To these we shall refer presently.

Application for discharge. Under the old Act, a bankrupt could apply for a discharge after the expiration of one month and within twelve months subsequent to adjudication. As a matter of practice the bankrupt, choosing his own time in applying for a discharge, often waited until creditors had lost interest, so that there would be little chance of opposition. The Bankruptcy Act now provides that the adjudication itself operates as an automatic application for a discharge. After the bankrupt has been examined, the court itself fixes the time for filing objections to a discharge. Notice must be given to all interested parties. The 

United States Attorney, when requested by the court, must examine into any case to ascertain whether there are probable grounds for denying a discharge. If he finds such grounds, he must oppose the discharge. Moreover, under the present Act, it is the duty of the trustee to "oppose at the expense of estates the discharges of bankrupts when they deem it advisable to do so." Too often, under the old Act, there was real ground for opposing a discharge, but creditors would either lose interest, or be unwilling to assume the cost of opposing a discharge, or else file objections and then, upon receiving some special inducement, drop the objections and let the discharge "slide through." This is precisely the situation sought to be cured under the new Act. 

In In re Simms, 26 Fed. Supp. 933 (D.C., W.Va.), the 

Workmen's Finance Corporation, a creditor, filed objections to 57

BANKRUPTCY 

the discharge of a bankrupt on the ground that he had obtained credit from such creditor by a false financial statement. 

The special master to whom these objections were referred reported that the bankrupt had given the creditor a new note for the debt and that the objecting creditor desired its objections withdrawn. 

No other creditor having opposed the discharge, the referee recommended that objections be withdrawn. The court, however, observed that this was precisely the situation intended to be covered by the provision of the Act of 1938 (section 14, subd. d) that upon request of the court it is the duty of the United States attorney to "examine into the acts and conduct of the bankrupt and, if satisfied that probable grounds exist for the denial of the discharge and that the public interest so warrants, he shall oppose the discharge of such bankrupt in like manner as provided in the case of a trustee." The court therefore withheld action on the petition for a discharge until such an investigation could be made. 

Said the court: "It would seem that small loan companies such as the Workmen's Finance Corporation would be vitally interested in the prosecution of objections to discharge in cases of this kind. If interest is lost as soon as their debts are satisfied, it is the duty of the court to protect the interest of all parties by requesting investigation of the acts of fraud described in the finance company's sworn specifications of objection to discharge. If the charges against bankrupt are false, he should be exonerated; if they are true, his discharge should be denied, in the interest of justice irrespective of the fact that the creditor making the charges of fraud is no longer interested in prosecuting its objections."

The foregoing rules under which the court takes the initiative in bringing on for a hearing the matter of a bankrupt's discharge, are not applicable to corporate bankrupts. A corporation may file an application for a discharge within six months after its adjudication.

Acts which will bar discharge: in general. A bankrupt will be unable to obtain his discharge in bankruptcy if it can be shown that he has been guilty of any of the following acts: (1) He has committed a bankruptcy offense; (2) He has destroyed, mutilated, falsified, concealed or 58

failed to keep books of account or records from which his financial condition and business transactions might be ascertained; (3) He has obtained money, property or credit by a false financial statement; (4) He has transferred, removed, destroyed or concealed any of his property within twelve months prior to the filing of the petition, with intent to hinder, delay or defraud creditors; (5) He has been granted a discharge or has had a composition or arrangement confirmed by a bankruptcy court within six years prior to bankruptcy; (6) (7) He has refused to obey a court order, or to answer a material question approved by the court in the course of a bankruptcy proceeding; or

He has failed to explain satisfactorily a loss or deficiency of assets. (1) Commission of bankruptcy offenses. 

The Bankruptcy

Act provides that the court shall grant a discharge unless satisfied that the bankrupt has "committed an offense punishable by imprisonment as provided under this Act."

The offenses punishable by imprisonment are referred to in Chapter 12. As a rule, such offenses must be knowingly and deliberately committed. As an example, we may take the offense of making false oath during the course of a bankruptcy proceeding. 

Such oath, to constitute a bar to discharge, must not only be false, but it must have been knowingly or intentionally fraudulent, Hence a false oath made through mistake, ignorance, inadvertance or misunderstanding will not prevent a discharge, because such oath could not have been knowingly or fraudulently made. The oath in such cases is false merely because it consists of swearing to something that is not a fact. If, for example, a bankrupt omits a debt from his schedules, and such omission was caused by mistake or inadvertance, it will not bar a discharge, as the court held in the case of In re Crenshaw, 95 Fed. 632 (D.C., Ala.). Likewise, in another case, a bankrupt druggist failed to include his brother as a creditor in his schedules, under the misapprehension that he was supposed to list only merchandise creditors. The court held that the omission did not constitute such false oath as 59

BANKRUPTCY 

would bar a discharge. Sharcoff v. Schieffelin & Co., 70 Fed. (2d) 

725 (C.C.A., N.Y.). 

A bankrupt (2) Destruction etc., of accounts or records. will be denied his discharge if the court is satisfied that he has destroyed, mutilated, falsified, concealed, or failed to keep or preserve books of account or records from which his financial condition and business transactions might be ascertained, unless in the opinion of the court such act or failure was justified by the circumstances. This means that in determining whether a bankrupt was justified in destroying or failing to keep records, each case must stand on its own facts.

This principle is illustrated by the case of Rosenberg v. 

Bloom, 99 Fed. (2d) 249 (C.C.A., Cal.). The facts are stated in the opinion of the court: "It appears from the statement of evidence that between the years 1919 and 1926 the bankrupt received as loans, large sums of money from one D. C. Bloom. 

These advances totaled between $140,000 and $160,- 000. This money was used by the bankrupt to purchase properties for himself, and in lending money on property. No written evidence of indebtedness was exchanged between the parties until May 1, 1930, when, after prolonged negotiations, the bankrupt entered into an agreement with D. C. Bloom by which the amount to be repaid to D. C. Bloom (and to another) for moneys advanced was fixed, and which provided for the conveyance of nine parcels of property to the California Pacific Title & Trust Company in trust to secure the indebtedness. The obligation thus evidenced has never been repaid, and the properties securing it are valueless. It represents a large portion of the indebtedness from which bankrupt now seeks to be discharged. 

The objecting creditors are nephews of D. C. Bloom and inherited his claim upon his death in 1932. "It further appears that at least until 1930 or thereabouts, the bankrupt was engaged on a substantial scale in the real estate business, in buying and selling, and in making loans. Since 1930 the bankrupt had only about three real estate transactions, and has engaged in no other business activity. The bankrupt filed his petition 60

in bankruptcy on January 30, 1936, and was adjudicated a bankrupt on the following day. "The only written record furnished by the bankrupt was a memorandum book containing notations made in January of 1936. The bankrupt testified that he destroyed the original memoranda from which this 'record' was made up. It purports to contain a record of practically all the bankrupt's transactions since May 1, 1930. The earliest transaction noted is indicated to have occurred June 14, 1929. The notes in the book make reference to various events and obligations, and are not in chronological order. The bankrupt furnished no records of his business transactions for the period prior to June 14, 1929. His testimony as to such records was that he kept a record of transactions with 

D. C. Bloom prior to 1930, but that after May 1, 1930, there was no use for them, and that he kept some and destroyed others; that he now has no records of such period. Bankrupt also testified that he kept a record of the transactions in two 'memo books,' but that he does not know what happened to them. Again, the bankrupt testified that in moving from hotel to hotel he gradually disposed of his records which were kept in a suit case, in order not to have to walk around with the suitcase in his hand. "In this state of the record, we believe that the finding of the Court 'that the bankrupt destroyed or failed to keep books of account or records from which his financial condition and business transactions might be ascertained, and (that) such failure or acts (are) not justified under all the circumstances of the case' is fully supported by the evidence. "It being obvious that the bankrupt's financial condition and business transactions could not be ascertained from the single record book which he did produce, and he having failed to justify his failure to produce adequate records, the judgment of the District Court denying the discharge must be affirmed." (3) False financial statements. The Act bars a discharge if the bankrupt has "obtained money or property on credit, or 61

BANKRUPTCY 

obtained an extension or renewal of credit, by making or publishing or causing to be made or published in any manner whatsoever, a materially false statement in writing respecting his financial condition." The courts have held that such statements, to bar a discharge, must have been known to be false, or must have been made recklessly without honest belief in their truth. Moreover, they must have been intended to deceive, and such deception must have been effectively relied upon. Protestations of innocence as to the contents of a statement are viewed with suspicion, especially where the bankrupt should have kown the contents. 

In In re Savarese (Appeal of State Bank), 209 Fed. 830 (C.C.A., N.Y.), an objecting creditor, the State Bank, appealed from an order granting a discharge. The bankrupt, Ferdinando 

Savarese, was the surviving member of a firm. His co-partner, a brother, had died some time before. The bankrupt's nephew,

Salvatore, had remained in the business as an inside office clerk, or bookkeeper, and it was he who had submitted the false financial statement. His uncle, the bankrupt, protested innocence as to this statement, and the special commissioner, who examined him, believed him, and recommended discharge. The district court confirmed the commissioner's report. The Circuit Court of Appeals reversed. The facts as given in the opinion of the Circuit Court of Appeals, and its findings on these facts, are of interest: "Vincenzo Savarese, one of the partners, died some years before, and his interest in the firm was never settled up.

His wife was administratrix of his estate, and his son Salvatore was a clerk in the employment of the firm on a weekly salary, and so remained down to the time of the adjudication. At that time he was about 22 years of age, while the bankrupt was 60 years of age, and had been in the business 40 years. The testimony is that Salvatore's very responsible duties were principally in the office, while Ferdinando's principal activities were outside, buying and selling. "July 16, 1909, Salvatore presented a written, but unsigned, statement of the firm's condition as of December 

31, 1908, to the State Bank, and on July 27, 1909, the bank advanced the firm $10,000 against warehouse receipts for oil and cheese. We think the statement was made for the purpose of obtaining credit from the bank, and that the bank relied upon it in making the advance of $10,000 to the firm. It is objected that this advance was not upon 62

credit, because the bank received the warehouse receipts as collateral. But the act does not say that the credit must be obtained solely on the written statement. We think it enough that the advance would not have been made without the statement. So treated, the loan was a credit within the meaning of the act. "The statement was false in various particulars. It included premises 95 First Place, at a value of $7,500, and 

115 First Place, at a value of $7,000. Neither of these premises was firm assets. The first belonged to the estate of 

Vincenzo Savarese, and the second to Ferdinando Savarese, who conveyed it to his wife in March, 1909. Moreover, the premises were included at full value, without deducting a mortgage of $5,000 on 95 First Place and of $5,500 on 115 First Place. An expert who examined the firm's books testified that the amount of notes payable was $74,367.82 instead of $50,302.36, and of accounts payable $51,383.80 instead of $34,590.78, as set out in the statement. In addition he found accounts payable to the amount of $5,- 

397.84 which were not on the ledger at all. "October 20, 1909, Salvatore Savarese presented a statement of the firm's affairs as of the same date (December 31, 1908) to one Dockendorff as a preliminary to an arrangement whereby all the firm's accounts receivable were to be assigned to him against advances of 75 per cent of their face. This statement made the accounts receivable $10,000 less, and the liabilities $17,000 greater, than did the statement submitted to the State Bank. It furthermore appeared that a separate ledger known as the fake ledger was kept of accounts receivable assigned to Dockendorff, many of which, as we understand it, were fictitious, though the evidence upon this point is meager. "No question is made but what these fraudulent acts and practices would bar the bankrupt's right to a discharge if he knew of them. He admits that he knew Salvatore prepared such statements, was authorized to do so, and that copies were kept which he saw. As matter of law, these acts of Salvatore were his, but as matter of morals, he says he thought the statement correct, and that, his duties being outside the office, he relied implicitly on Salvatore, the bookkeeper and other office clerks, who were acting in the premises. 63

BANKRUPTCY 

The special commissioner was so favorably impressed by the appearance and manner of Ferdinando Savarese in testifying that he believed him. The district judge, with hesitation, confirmed the report. "Giving great weight, as we should, to these conclusions, we are still unable to believe that Ferdinando Savarese was without knowledge in the premises. He had been 40 years in the business, and was entirely familiar with the amount and value of goods on hand. He could hardly have overlooked the fact that his First Place premises were included in the statement as firm assets, and were grossly overvalued. 

At the least, he was recklessly negligent. If such accounts as his are to be adopted, it will be made very easy for embarrassed merchants to close their eyes to fraudulent acts of their servants by which they profit, and then, if bankruptcy ensues, obtain the discharge which is intended to enable the merely unfortunate debtors to start life anew. "The order is reversed." (4) Removing, destroying or concealing property. The Act bars discharge to a bankrupt who has at any time within twelve months immediately preceding the filing of the petition "transferred, removed, destroyed, or concealed, or permitted to be removed, destroyed or concealed, any of his property, with intent to hinder, delay, or defraud his creditors." 

In In re Sorkowitz, 19 Fed. Supp. 723 (D.C., N.Y.), the bankrupts, on the eve of an assignment for the benefit of creditors, withdrew $200 and $400 from a concern which was collecting their accounts receivable, without making any entry on their books. 

This was some two months before the filing of a petition in bankruptcy, and the court held that this constituted "concealment of money with intent to defraud their creditors," and hence that they were not entitled to a discharge. 

When in doubt, a bankrupt will be wise if he makes full disclosure, even if such disclosure reveals previous efforts at concealment.

In In re Doody, 92 Fed. (2d) 653 (C.C.A., Ill.), a bankrupt, without receiving any consideration, had conveyed real property to a dummy, who subsequently conveyed the property to the bankrupt's wife and daughter. This was over a year before the filing of the bankruptcy petition. The bankrupt, however, at the first meeting of creditors and at the hearing before the referee on objections to his discharge, fully disclosed the entire trans- 64

action and submitted the various deeds for inspection. The court held that the bankrupt was not guilty of such fraudulent concealment as would bar his discharge, although the deeds to the wife and daughter had never been recorded. (5) Prior discharge or composition. The Act bars a discharge if the bankrupt "has within six years prior to bankruptcy been granted a discharge, or had a composition or an arrangement by way of composition or a wage earners' plan by way of composition confirmed under this Act." Such prior discharge or composition will constitute a bar whether the subsequent bankruptcy proceedings are voluntary or involuntary. (6) Refusal to obey court order or answer material question.

The Act bars a discharge to a bankrupt if in the course of a proceeding he has "refused to obey any lawful order of, or to answer any material question approved by, the court.' 

Refusal to obey a "lawful order" of the court has been interpreted to mean not only the usual mandates of a court, but even an ordinary subpoena. In In re Simon, 297 Fed. 942 (C.C.A., N.Y.), the court held that a refusal by the bankrupt to obey a subpoena issued in the proceedings for which he was adjudged in contempt, was ground for denying his discharge. A subpoena, said the court, is a writ, and being mandatory, is a “lawful order of the court" within the meaning of the Bankruptcy Act. 

A refusal to answer a question is not ground for denying a discharge unless the question (a) was propounded in the course of a bankruptcy proceeding, (b) was material, and (c) was approved by the judge or referee. "Approval" does not mean formal approval: where counsel for the bankrupt objects to a question and the referee over-rules the objection, a refusal by the bankrupt to answer constitutes a bar to his discharge. In re Hochberg, 17 Fed. Supp. 916 (D.C., Pa.). (7) Failure to explain loss or deficiency. The Act bars a discharge to a bankrupt if he “has failed to explain satisfactorily any losses of assets or deficiencies of assets to meet his liabilities." 

In In re Beckman, 6 Fed. Supp. 957 (D.C., N.Y.), the bankrupt sought to explain away a large portion of his loss by stating that he sold goods at less than wholesale cost over a period of a year because it was necessary to meet competition, and that $5,000 was lost in this manner. Said the court: "The only basis for arriving at this amount was that the bankrupt concluded he had lost about 20 to 25 per cent on the $28,000 worth of goods which he turned over during the year. In view of the fact that at best this can be regarded only as an estimate by the bankrupt. 65

BANKRUPTCY 

founded upon nothing which in any way verifies or affirms it, the explanation cannot be regarded as satisfactory." 

In In re Margolis, 23 Fed. Supp. 735 (D.C., N.Y.), a loss of assets during an accounting period ran to $38,000 and the deficiency in assets to $20,000. The bankrupt sought to explain the shrinkage by stating that a robbery had occurred. But without supporting proof, the court refused to accept the explanation as satisfactory: "There was no effort by the bankrupt to establish the quantity or the value of furs or of any other property abstracted and carried away by the robbers on September 21. Standing alone, is not the recited feature of the proof enough to compel the court to say that there is reasonable cause to believe that the bankrupt did not make a satisfactory explanation . . .? I think it is."

Waiver of right to discharge. A bankrupt is deemed to have waived his right to a discharge, and an order may be entered to this effect, if— (a) He fails to appear at the hearing on his discharge; or (b) Having appeared, he refuses to submit himself to examination; or (c) Without sufficient excuse, he has failed to appear and submit himself to examination at the first meeting of creditors or at any meeting specially called for his examination. 

Debts: dischargeability as dependent on provability. A discharge in bankruptcy releases the bankrupt from all próvable debts, but it does not release him from non-provable debts. There is no reason why a bankrupt should be discharged from debts not provable at the time of bankruptcy. Such a discharge would unduly prejudice the creditor, because he would then not only be unable to prove his claim in bankruptcy, but he would likewise be unable to collect it after the discharge in bankruptcy. 

Debts which are provable in bankruptcy have already been discussed in Chapter 8. In the same chapter we observed how, under the old law, unexpired leases were formerly not provable, hence not dischargeable, in bankruptcy, a situation that no longer obtains under the present Act.

We also noted, in the preceding chapter, that provable debts included a fixed liability, evidenced by a written instrument, absolutely owing, though not yet due, at the time of the filing of 66

the petition. Such a liability, being provable, is dischargeable. If, therefore, such a claim is not proved in the bankruptcy proceeding, it is lost. For example, a promissory note may have three years more to run at the time of the filing of the petition. If the holder on that account fails to file his proof of claim, but waits until the note is due, and in the meantime the bankrupt maker has been discharged, the holder will be unable to recover on his note.

The most common instance of a debt not provable, hence not dischargeable in bankruptcy, is one arising out of goods sold, money loaned or services rendered after the commencement of a bankruptcy proceeding.

As an illustration we may take the case of In re Burka, 104 Fed. 326 (D.C., Mo.). In that case attorney's fees were incurred after the filing of a petition and before adjudication. These fees were for services in no way related to the bankruptcy. The court held that since the obligation for these services was not provable in the bankruptcy proceeding, it was not dischargeable, either, and the obligation survived the bankrupt's discharge. 

Provable debts which are not dischargeable. Not all debts which are provable are necessarily on that account dischargeable. 

The following classes of provable debts are not dischargeable in bankruptcy, and will survive as obligations notwithstanding the bankrupt's discharge: (1) Taxes; (2) Liabilities for obtaining money or property by false pretenses or representations; (3) Liabilities for wilful and malicious injuries to the person or property of another; (4) Liabilities for alimony due or to become due; (5) Liabilities for maintenance or support of a wife or child; (6) Liabilities for seduction, or for breach of promise of marriage accompanied by seduction, or for criminal conversation; (7) Debts not scheduled by the bankrupt, owed to a creditor ignorant of the bankruptcy proceedings; (8) Debts created by the bankrupt's fraud, embezzlement, misappropriation or defalcation while acting as an officer or in any fiduciary capacity; 67

BANKRUPTCY 

(9) Debts due for wages which have been earned within three months before the date of commencement of the bankruptcy proceedings; and (10) Debts due employees for monies deposited by them as security for faithful performance.

Some of these non-dischargeable obligations are deserving of special comment.

Taxes. The Act provides that taxes shall not be dischargeable in bankruptcy. These include not only Federal taxes, but also taxes levied by any state, county, district or municipality. 

Obtaining money or property by false pretenses. Liabilities of a bankrupt "for obtaining money or property by false pretenses or false representations" are not dischargeable in bankruptcy. 

In Rowell v. Ricker, 79 Vt. 552 (66 Atl. 569), the bankrupt obtained merchandise on credit by falsely stating to the seller that a certain check which he was expecting from a customer had already been mailed, and that he would turn the check over to the seller immediately upon its receipt. Such a false statement, said the court, constituted obtaining property by false pretenses, and the obligation thereby incurred was not dischargeable in bankruptcy. 

In Ehlinger v. Spreckles, 189 S.W. 348 (Tex. Civ. App.), the bankrupt induced the making of a loan by falsely representing himself to be the owner of an insurance policy worth $500, when in fact the full loan value of such policy had been taken up, a fact which was not disclosed. The court held that the obligation thereby incurred was not dischargeable in bankruptcy. 

Must the false representation have been made in writing in order to render the obligation not dischargeable? The court in 

Katzenstein v. Reid & Co., 41 Tex. Civ. App. 106, 91 S.W. 

360 answers this question in the negative. The court in the same case held that the representation need not have been made to the particular creditor seeking to have the debt excluded from the discharge; that where the statement was made to a mercantile agency instead of to such creditor, it was sufficient to render the obligation non-dischargeable.

Wilful and malicious injuries to person or property. The Act provides that a discharge in bankruptcy will not release the bankrupt from a liability incurred "for wilful and malicious injuries to the person or property of another." For example, a judgment for assault and battery would constitute a liability incurred for a 68

wilful and malicious injury to the person of another. The deliberate poisoning of a neighbor's dog would constitute wilful and malicious injury to the property of another.

In In re Papale, 17 Fed. Supp. 146 (D.C., N.Y.), the bankrupt permitted his thirteen and a half year old son to use a B-B gun in shooting at cats, birds and his own playmates, in consequence of which one of his playmates lost the sight of one eye. 

The court held that this was sufficient to show malice and to render the liability not dischargeable in bankruptcy. 

Liabilities for alimony, maintenance or support of wife or child. 

Claims for alimony, or for maintenance or support of a wife or child, either due or to become due, are not affected by a discharge in bankruptcy. Claims of this character concern not only the parties themselves, but the state as well. Family obligations are not mere matters of contract. The rights, duties and obligations arising out of the family relationship have a direct bearing on the public welfare. Hence public policy requires that such obligations should not be dischargeable in bankruptcy.

Debts not duly scheduled. The Act provides that debts shall not be dischargeable in bankruptcy if they "have not been duly scheduled in time for proof and allowance, with the name of the creditor, if known to the bankrupt, unless such creditor had notice or actual knowledge of the proceedings in bankruptcy." If a creditor has actual knowledge of proceedings in bankruptcy, he must file his proof of claim, whether his name is listed in the schedules or not. The only way he could be prejudiced by a failure to list his name in the schedules would be by knowledge of the bankruptcy coming too late to permit the creditor to file his proof of claim within the time allowed by law.
