The bankrupt in preparing his schedules should be careful to designate, as accurately as possible, not only the names of creditors, but their addresses as well; and in designating the names and addresses of his creditors, it will not do for the bankrupt to be deliberately vague or misleading. Particularly is this true where it can be shown that the bankrupt had knowledge in respect to which he was thus evasive.

In Miller v. Guasti, 226 U.S. 170, the bankrupt in his schedules set forth that the residence and occupation of Guasti and Bernard, holders of a certain judgment against him, were: "Unknown-California." Said the court: "In view of the facts found, it is manifest that the debt to Guasti and Bernard was not scheduled as it should have been in order to make the discharge operative as to it. Their residence, though found to be known, 69

BANKRUPTCY 

was not stated, as required by the Act, and the creditor did not have actual knowledge of the bankruptcy proceedings until long after their termination." 

In In re Cleveland, 30 Fed. Supp. 868 (D.C., N.Y.), a creditor was listed by its wrong corporate name. It was held that the debt thus designated was not properly scheduled, and was not released by the bankrupt's discharge in bankruptcy. 

Debts created by fraud, embezzlement, etc. in official or fiduciary capacity. The Act renders non-dischargeable all debts of a bankrupt which "were created by his fraud, embezzlement, misappropriation or defalcation while acting as an officer or in any fiduciary capacity." One or two cases will indicate the nature of this type of non-dischargeable liability. 

In Donahue v. Conley, 85 Cal. App. 15, 258 P. 985, the defendant gave cash and a note for a sum embezzled. He then gave a new note for the unpaid portion of the old one. The question arose as to whether this latter note was dischargeable in bankruptcy, and the court held that the original fraud precluded a discharge of this obligation. 

In National Surety Co. v. Wittich, 185 Minn. 321, 240

N.W. 888, a postmaster failed to account for and pay over government funds while in control of the post office. The court held that this constituted a defalcation under the statute and rendered the obligation not dischargeable in bankruptcy. 

In In re Bernard, 87 Fed. (2d) 705 (C.C.A., N.Y.), the officer of a corporation, knowing that the corporation was insolvent, transferred assets to himself and to his son to liquidate their own claims against the corporation, in violation of the New York statute. The court held that the liability thus incurred represented a “misappropriation while acting as officer" within the meaning of the Bankruptcy Act, and that such liability was not dischargeable in bankruptcy. 

In Orndorff v. State ex rel. McGill, 108 S.W. (2d) 206 (Tex. Civ. App.), a judgment was rendered in favor of a county and against its sheriff, for profits made out of government monies entrusted to the sheriff for the safekeeping, care and feeding of Federal prisoners. Subsequently, and without payment of this judgment, the sheriff procured a discharge in bankruptcy. The court held that such discharge did not extinguish the county's judgment, because the failure to pay over the amount of such judgment constituted a "defalcation." 

In Bannon v. Knauss, 57 Ohio App. 288, 13 N.E. (2d) 

733, the court held that the word "misappropriation" did not 70

necessarily involve criminal conduct, and that the act of a corporate treasurer in overdrawing his account with his company for his personal benefit, and in refusing on demand to make restitution to the company, constituted a "misappropriation" within the meaning of the Bankruptcy Act, and hence was not dischargeable in bankruptcy.

Revival of debt discharged in bankruptcy. The rule is general that a subsequent or new promise in writing to pay a debt discharged in bankruptcy revives the debt. Where a person orally agrees to revive a debt discharged in bankruptcy and then gives a note pursuant to such agreement, the Statute of Frauds is satisfied, because the note would represent a promise in writing to pay a debt discharged in bankruptcy, and would be enforcible. Stokes v. Sanders, 181 App. Div. 249, 168 N.Y. Supp. 409.

Revocation of discharge. The Bankruptcy Act provides (sec. 

15) that "The court may, upon the application of parties in interest who have not been guilty of undue laches, filed at any time within one year after a discharge shall have been granted, revoke it if it shall be made to appear that it was obtained through the fraud of the bankrupt, that the knowledge of the fraud has come to the petitioners since the granting of the discharge and that the actual facts did not warrant the discharge." 71

CHAPTER 10 CORPORATE REORGANIZATION 

Definition and purpose. The term "reorganization,” literally, means to organize anew. As applied to corporations, the terms contemplates a rearrangement of the interests of creditors and security holders, through the formation of a new corporation organized to take over the business of the old. The purpose of a corporate reorganization is to scale down capital obligations at a minimum sacrifice to stockholders, bondholders and unsecured creditors, so as to permit the corporation to continue in business, instead of "folding up" with the usual losses entailed by a forced liquidation.

Corporate reorganization outside the Bankruptcy Act. Corporate relief through reorganization is by no means new. Courts of equity, through the medium of equity receivership, have provided this form of relief for many years. Voluntary reorganizations with the consent of all parties interested, including stockholders and creditors, have likewise been available in times of financial distress. These forms of relief, however, have proved far from satisfactory. The necessity of procuring unanimous consent, the temptation, open to any creditor, to capitalize on the fact that he could not be compelled to consent to a reorganization, and the excessive fees, expenses, and delays of equity receivership have frequently proved insuperable obstacles to corporate reorganization outside the Bankruptcy Act.

Corporate reorganization through compositions in bankruptcy. 

Corporate reorganizations have also been available for many years under the Bankruptcy Act; but the procedure and form of relief hitherto available were radically different from those available under the present Act. Under the old Act, and prior to the adoption of former section 77B, corporations, after the filing of a petition, could effect a reorganization through the medium of a composition, either with or without an adjudication in bankruptcy. Where a corporation desired such reorganization, the practice was to file a petition in bankruptcy, and then to propose a composition with creditors. If the composition was approved by the 72

CORPORATE REORGANIZATION 

creditors and confirmed by the court, the corporation would sell all of its assets, including, usually, its name and good will, to a newly organized corporation, which often bore the old corporate name, and which was organized with a corporate set-up to conform to the composition agreement and the scheme of reorganization as originally developed. Usually, capital stock of the new corporation was issued to pay the "composition dividend" to creditors who had agreed to accept the composition. The cash necessary for administration expenses, and to pay creditors unwilling to accept stock, might be provided through the sale of treasury stock of the new corporation, or through the issuance of bonds, or in some other manner.

Corporate reorganizations under "77B". In the years which followed the financial panic of 1929, the need for corporate reorganizations became exceptionally acute. Economic stability was threatened by the gaunt array of countless insolvent corporations facing the usual alternative of bankruptcy or receivership, with the inevitable sale of corporate assets at distress prices,-yielding slim proceeds to creditors and stockholders, and threatening to disorganize values by wholesale dumping. The 1934 amendment to the 

Bankruptcy Act, embracing the addition of section 77B, sought to give corporations an opportunity to scale down their capital structures and reduce top-heavy capital obligations. The idea was, that by saving a going business-at least in part-to creditors, stockholders and the community, everybody would benefit. 

The aims, purposes and essential framework of former section 

77B have been summarized as follows (8 Corpus Juris Secundum 

(1) To provide a plan which, on approval by the court and acceptance by a majority of the parties interested, would be compulsory; (2) To rehabilitate corporations solvent in fact but unable to meet maturing obligations, and thereby to avoid that dismemberment of the interests of stockholders and creditors which other remedies frequently entailed; (3) To preserve a going concern, and to keep the business intact; (4) To permit continuance on a sounder business basis, by scaling down capital obligations without undue injury to the rights of stockholders and creditors; 73

BANKRUPTCY 

(5) To afford greater dispatch, economy and simplicity, as against the cumbersomeness, delays and expense of other forms of reorganization; and (6) To provide relief against recalcitrant minority, creditors.

Deficiencies of reorganization under "77B". Desirable in theory as the above objectives were, in practice the administration of corporate reorganizations under former section 77B revealed serious abuses. Under the guise of seeking a "reorganization," corporations frequently sought to evade legitimate obligations, and secured numerous and unwarranted delays and postponements without formulating any definite plan. Reorganization proceedings were often controlled by inside groups. Those in control usually and naturally failed to reveal and punish acts of corporate mismanagement and diversion of corporate assets. Minority investment interests were largely ignored. Large fees for counsel, trustees and committees were built up and paid at the expense of creditors and stockholders. Numerous other abuses called for change.

Improvements under Bankruptcy Act of 1938. Among the numerous reforms embodied in the Bankruptcy Act of 1938, none received more thoughtful study than those governing corporate reorganizations as embodied in Chapter X of the Act. Outstanding characteristics of these changes are the following: (1) The appointment of a disinterested trustee, and the enlargement of his duties; (2) Requiring the court to pass on a reorganization plan before security holders are called upon to vote on it, and thereby eliminating the practice whereby committees of security holders competed for acceptances of plans of reorganization without prior approval by the court; (3) Invoking a report by the Securities and Exchange Commission as to a proposed plan of reorganization, before creditors and security holders are asked to cast their votes; (4) Making the trustee, where one is appointed, responsible in the first instance for drawing up a plan of reorganization: (5) Requiring a petition for reorganization to show specifi facts why adequate relief cannot be obtained by way of an arrangement, instead of by way of reorganization. 

In short, to use the language of the Senate Committee Report which approved the Bankruptcy Act of 1938, it was the object of 74

CORPORATE REORGANIZATION 

the Act, in dealing with corporate reorganizations, to insure that the court and all interested parties "be provided with unbiased and impartial information," and that "the crucial procedure of plan formulation take place inside, instead of outside the court.' 

Present framework and procedure for corporate reorganization. 

The framework and procedure for corporate reorganization under the Bankruptcy Act of 1938, briefly stated, are as follows: 

A. The jurisdiction and powers of the court during the pendency of the proceeding are the same, substantially, as those in an ordinary bankruptcy proceeding. B.

Proceedings are instituted by the filing of a petition. 

C. The petition may be controverted by an answer; but whether controverted or not, the petition must be passed upon by the court, who may approve or dismiss it. 

D. Upon approval of the petition, creditors and stockholders file their proofs of claim or interests in the manner and within the time fixed by the judge.

E. Upon approving the petition, the judge must arrange for intermediate possession, custody and management of the corporate debtor's business, either by appointing a trustee or trustees, or by allowing the debtor to remain in possession subject to specified safeguards and court supervision.

F. A plan of reorganization is then formulated and filed, which must contain certain provisions, and may contain others, tending to insure a fair, equitable and feasible program of adjustment.

G. Hearings are then held on the plan, with a view to court approval; and the judge approves the plan or plans which in his opinion comply with the requirements of the Act, and are fair, equitable and feasible.

H. The plan or plans approved are then submitted for acceptance by creditors and stockholders.

I. Upon acceptance, the plan must be confirmed by the court. 

J. Upon confirmation, the plan is consummated by the liquidations and distributions called for under the plan. 

K. If a plan fails of formulation, approval, acceptance, confirmation or consummation, the judge dismisses the petition, adjudi- 75

BANKRUPTCY 

cates the debtor a bankrupt, or directs the bankruptcy proceeding to continue, as the case may be.

A. Jurisdiction and Powers of Court 

Status of debtor upon filing of petition. Upon the filing of a petition for reorganization, and pending its approval, the jurisdiction, powers and duties of the court are the same as they are in ordinary bankruptcy between the date of the filing of the petition and the date of the adjudication (see Chapter 5). The filing of the petition gives the court immediate jurisdiction over the corporations' property wherever located.

Stays and injunctions. Either before or after the approval of the petition, the judge may enjoin any proceeding affecting the debtor's property. It may stay any party or any action, at any stage. 

In County of Cook v. Straus, 78 Fed. (2d) 491 (C.C.A.,

Ill), a county bought in the debtor's property at a tax sale. The 

Circuit Court of Appeals upheld the right of the bankruptcy court to stay the county from further proceedings to obtain a tax deed, inasmuch as the debtor still had a right to redeem the property before the deed was actually obtained. 

The power of the bankruptcy court to enjoin all further proceedings affecting the debtor's property may even extend to a foreign jurisdiction, where the representatives from such foreign jurisdiction file an appearance in the reorganization proceeding. 

In Detroit Trust Co. v. Campbell River Timber Co., Ltd., 98 Fed. (2d) 389 (C.C.A., Wash.), a trustee under an indenture of trust covering certain timberlands in British Columbia, together with a person acting on his behalf, appeared in a reorganization proceeding in the Federal Court in the State of Washington. The court held that even though the property affected was outside the country, the court had jurisdiction to issue an injunction restraining the indenture trustee from commencing or prosecuting a foreclosure proceeding in British Columbia, where the property was situated.

Power to reject executory contracts. 

We have already pointed out in Chapter 8 that under the Act of 1938, a trustee in bankruptcy may assume or reject any executory contracts, including unexpired leases, whichever course he deems to be in the interests of the estate. Similarly, upon the filing of a petition for reorgani- 76

CORPORATE REORGANIZATION 

zation, the judge may permit the rejection of all executory contracts except those in the public authority. Of course this does not apply to the obligations incurred under a contract to the extent that it has been executed.

It is not always easy, however, to determine whether a contract is executory, hence subject to rejection, or executed and not subject to rejection. This is particularly true where a contract has been executed, but items of performance remain to be completed in the future. Suppose, for example, that a corporation assigns future income, such as, say, rentals to become due under a lease. 

Suppose that thereafter the corporation files a petition for reorganization. May the debtor's trustee reject so much of the assignment as applies to the rents not yet paid? That is the question which arose in In re H. K. Porter Co., 24 Fed. Supp. 766 (D.C., Pa.). The debtor corporation had made an assignment of rents. to a mortgagee. After the filing of a petition for reorganization it was sought to reject this assignment as an executory contract, at least to the extent of the unpaid rentals. The debtor's position was, that the contract was executory to the extent of the unpaid rentals, hence subject to disaffirmance. The court, however, held differently: "We cannot so regard the assignment. So far as the debtor is concerned, it is an executed contract. The trustees admit the contract is executed, so far as concerns the rents which have already been paid, but not as to rents still to accrue. We do not see how the debtor can play fast and loose as to his assignment. Either it assigned the rents, or it did not. As we view the paper, the assignment is absolute and cannot now be set aside at the option of the trustees."

Referees; special masters; receivers. Under section 117 of the present Bankruptcy Act, the judge may refer a reorganization at any stage to a referee in bankruptcy, "to hear and determine" any and all matters not reserved by law to the judge himself; or he may refer such matters to a special master (who may also be a referee), "to hear and report." The difference between such references is that the findings of the referee require no confirmation, and in the absence of appeal, are final; whereas the report of a special master, to be effective, must be confirmed.

A receiver in a reorganization proceeding may also be appointed by the judge. 77

BANKRUPTCY 

Powers reserved to bankruptcy judge. Specific powers reserved to the bankruptcy judge are: (1) (2) (3) (4) (5) (6) Power to authorize rejection of executory contracts; 

Authority to issue receiver's and trustee's certificates; 

Authorization to dispose of the debtor's property; 

Power to enjoin pending suits;

Power to refer matters to a referee in bankruptcy; 

Power to refer matters to a special master; and (7) Power to transfer proceedings to another judicial district.

B. The Petition 

Who may file petition. A petition for reorganization may be filed either by the corporation itself, or by three or more creditors of the corporation who have claims aggregating $5,000 or over, provided such claims are liquidated in amount and not contingent as to liability. The petition may also be filed by an indenture trustee, provided the securities outstanding under the indenture are liquidated in amount and not contingent as to liability. The 

Act as amended in 1938 prevents a multiplicity of petitions by providing that a petition may be filed only "if no other petition by or against such corporation is pending under this chapter." 

Petitions for reorganization in existing bankruptcy proceedings. 

A petition for reorganization may be filed by or against a corporation during the pendency of a bankruptcy proceeding affecting such corporation. Such petition may be filed either before or after the corporation has been adjudicated a bankrupt. 

Pendency of prior mortgage foreclosure or other proceeding. 

A petition for reorganization may be filed notwithstanding the pendency of a prior mortgage foreclosure, or of a prior equity or other proceeding in any state or Federal court in which a receiver or trustee has been applied for, or has even been appointed, for all or any part of the debtor's property. The commencement of reorganization proceedings supersedes all other such proceedings. 

Contents of petition by debtor. Every petition for reorganization filed by a debtor corporation must state: (1) That the corporation is insolvent or unable to pay its debts as they mature; 78

CORPORATE REORGANIZATION 

(2) Facts showing that the court has jurisdiction over the debtor corporation; (3) The nature of the business of the corporation; (4) The assets, liabilities, capital stock and financial condition of the corporation; (5) The nature of all pending proceedings affecting the property of the corporation, and the courts in which they are pending; (6) The status of any pending plan of reorganization, readjustment or liquidation affecting the property of the corporation, either with or without judicial proceeding; (7) The specific facts showing the need for relief by way of reorganization, and why adequate relief cannot be obtained by an arrangement; and (8) The petitioner's desire that a plan of reorganization be effected. Where

Contents of petition by creditors or indenture trustees. petitions for reorganization are filed by creditors or indenture trustees, instead of by the debtor, the petition, in addition to the facts which must be set forth in a debtor's petition, must also show at least one of the following facts: (1) that the corporation was adjudged a bankrupt in a pending proceeding; or (2) that a receiver or trustee has been appointed for, or has taken charge of all or the greater portion of the property of the corporation in a pending equity proceeding; or (3) that an indenture trustee, or a mortgagee under a mortgage is, by reason of a default, in possession of all or the greater portion of the property of the corporation; or (4) that a proceeding to foreclose a mortgage or to enforce a lien against all or the greater portion of the property of the corporation is pending; or (5) that the corporation has committed an act of bankruptcy within four months prior to the filing of the petition. 

Service of copy of petition and subpoena on debtor. Upon the filing of a petition against a corporation, a copy of the petition, 79

BANKRUPTCY 

together with a subpoena returnable within ten days, or within such additional time as the court may allow, must be served upon the debtor corporation.

C. Approval or Dismissal of Petition: Answer 

In general. The petition for reorganization, whether filed by the debtor, creditors or indenture trustee, must be passed upon by the judge, who must approve or dismiss the petition. The petition as thus passed upon may be either controverted, or uncontroverted; that is, the debtor may have filed a petition, which may be controverted, or uncontroverted; or creditors or an indenture trustee may have filed a petition, which may be controverted, or uncontroverted.

How petitions controverted: answers. A petition is controverted by an answer. Answers may be filed by the debtor corporation itself, where the petition is filed against it, or by a creditor, indenture trustee or stockholder, where the petition is filed either by or against the debtor. A stockholder, however, may file an answer only where it is claimed that the debtor is solvent. 

We thus have the following situations in which a court may be called upon to approve or dismiss a petition for a reorganization: 

Petition filed by debtor, not controverted by answer. (1) (2) Petition filed by creditors or indenture trustee, not controverted by answer. 

Petition filed by debtor, controverted by answer. (4) Petition filed by creditors or indenture trustee, controverted by answer. 

Where the (1) Petition filed by debtor, not controverted. petition is filed by the debtor corporation, and is uncontroverted by an answer, the judge must enter an order approving the petition if he is satisfied (a) that it complies with the statute, and (b) that it has been filed in good faith; otherwise, he must dismiss the petition. (2) Petition filed by creditors or indenture trustee, not controverted by answer. Where the petition is filed against a debtor corporation, and the latter either files no answer, or an answer which does not controvert any material allegation in the petition, 80

CORPORATE REORGANIZATION 

the judge must enter an order approving the petition if he is satisfied (a) that it complies with the statute, and (b) that it has been filed in good faith; otherwise, he must dismiss the petition. (3) Petition filed by debtor, controverted by answer. Where the petition is filed by the debtor, an answer controverting the allegations of the petition may be filed at any time prior to the first date set for a hearing on the petition—a minimum period of thirty days. The answer may be filed by any creditor or indenture trustee, or if the debtor is alleged to be solvent-by any stockholder of the debtor corporation. In such case, the judge must as soon as possible determine the issues presented by the petition and answer. If he is satisfied (a) that the petition complies with the statute, (b) that it has been filed in good faith, and (c) that its material allegations have been sustained by the proofs, he must enter an order approving the petition; otherwise, he must dismiss it. (4) Petition filed by creditors or indenture trustee, controverted by answer. Where the petition is filed against the debtor corporation, the latter, within ten days after being served with the subpoena and a copy of the petition, or within such additional time as the court allows, may file an answer controverting the facts alleged in the petition. Likewise, at any time prior to the first day set for a hearing on the petition, any other creditor or indenture trustee, or any stockholder if it is alleged that the corporation is solvent, may file an answer controverting the allegations of the petition. The judge must as soon as possible determine the issues presented by the petition and answer, and if he is satisfied (a) that the petition complies with the statute, (b) that it has been filed in good faith, and (c) that its material allegations have been sustained by the proofs, he must enter an order approving the petition; otherwise, he must dismiss it. 

Good faith of petition. Not only must a petition be filed in good faith within the usual meaning of the term, but a petition is not deemed to be filed in good faith if— (1) the petitioning creditors have acquired their claims for the purpose of filing the petition; or (2) adequate relief would be obtainable by an arrangement instead of by a reorganization; or (3) it is unreasonable to expect that a plan of reorganization can be effected; or 81

BANKRUPTCY 

(4) a prior proceeding is pending in any court and it appears that the interests of creditors and stockholders would be best served in such prior proceeding. 

Petition must set forth financial structure. Courts are required to scrutinize a petition carefully before approving it, to make sure that it really sets forth the debtor's financial condition, its need for reorganization and the possibility of effecting a reorganization. 

As the court said in In re Suburban Properties, 110 Fed. (2d) 

438, in reversing an order of the District Court which approved a petition: "Nowhere in the record before this court is there the story of the debtor's financial condition, its need for reorganization, and the possibility of effecting the same." 

Reorganization proceeding converted into arrangement proceeding. Where it appears that a petition for reorganization is improper because adequate relief can be obtained by an arrangement proceeding, the petition may be amended, upon the application of the debtor, to comply with the requirements for the filing of an arrangement proceeding. Thereafter, the provisions of the Act governing arrangement proceedings will control all further steps. 

Approval of petition constitutes an automatic stay. Unless otherwise ordered by the judge, an order approving a reorganization petition operates automatically as a stay of any prior pending bankruptcy proceeding, mortgage foreclosure or equity receivership proceeding, and of any act or other proceeding to enforce a lien against the debtor's property.

D. Creditors, Stockholders and Proofs of Claim 

Time for filing and allowing proofs of claims; objections to allowances. After approving the petition, the judge must prescribe the manner and time for filing and allowing proofs of the claims and interests of creditors and stockholders. Objections by any party in interest to the allowance of such claims or interests must be heard and summarily determined by the court. 

Classification of creditors and stockholders. For the purposes of the plan and its acceptance, the judge must divide creditors and stockholders into classes according to the nature of their respective claims and stock.

United States as creditor. Claims of the United States as a secured or unsecured creditor, or as a stockholder, are subject to plans of reorganization, and the Secretary of the Treasury is authorized to accept or reject a plan on behalf of the United States. If 82

CORPORATE REORGANIZATION 

the United States is a creditor for unpaid taxes or customs duties, no plan can be confirmed by the judge which does not provide for payment of such claim, "except upon the acceptance of a lesser amount by the Secretary of the Treasury certified to the court." 

Rejection of executory contract or unexpired lease. Where an executory contract, is rejected on behalf of the debtor corporation, any person injured by such rejection is deemed a creditor, and may file a claim for the loss occasioned by the rejection. Where, however, an unexpired lease is rejected on behalf of the debtor corporation as tenant, the landlord's claim is limited to rent already accrued without acceleration, plus future rent for a period not exceeding three years after the premises were surrendered or the landlord made reentry, whichever occurred first. 

Representation in proceedings. 

Any creditor or stockholder may act personally in a corporate reorganization proceeding, or may be represented by an attorney at law, a duly authorized agent, or a committee. 

An attorney corporate reor- 

Attorney must file list of persons represented. for creditors or stockholders cannot be heard in ganization proceeding unless he has first filed with the court a statement setting forth the names and addresses of such creditors or stockholders, the nature and amounts of their claims or stock, and-if acquired within a year prior to the date of the filing of the petition—the time of such acquisition. 

Statement to be filed by certain representatives and indenture trustees. Every person or committee representing more than twelve creditors or stockholders, and every indenture trustee must file a sworn statement with the court, which must include: (1) A copy of the instrument, if any, by which such person, committee or trustee is authorized to act for such creditors or stockholders; (2) A recital of the pertinent facts in connection with the employment of such person or indenture trustee, or, in the case of a committee, the names of the persons at whose instance the employment was arranged or the committee organized; (3) Facts as to the time of employment of such person, the organization of such committee, or the appearance of the indenture trustee, showing amounts of claims or of stock owned by such persons, indenture trustees or committee members, the times when acquired, the amounts paid therefor, and any sales or other disposition of them; and (4) The respective amounts of claims or of stock repre- 83

BANKRUPTCY 

sented by such persons or committees, with an averment that each holder of such claims or stock acquired the same at least one year before the filing of the petition; otherwise, a showing as to the times when such claims or stock were acquired. 

E. Intermediate Possession, Custody and Management 

Trustees and debtors in possession. Upon approving a petition, the judge must arrange for intermediate possession, custody and management of the corporate debtor's business, pending the submission, approval, adoption and consummation of a plan of reorganization. If the debtor's indebtedness, liquidated as to amount and not contingent as to liability, is $250,000 or over, the judge must appoint one or more trustees, who must be "disinterested." If the debtor's indebtedness is less than $250,000, the judge may appoint a trustee, or he may, in his discretion, continue the debtor in possession, in which case the debtor's position and responsibility correspond to those of a trustee on behalf of creditors and stockholders.

Additional trustees for management purposes. Where a disinterested trustee is appointed, the judge may appoint an additional trustee for management purposes, who may be a director, officer or employee of the debtor corporation. 

Attorney for trustee. The attorney for the trustee must likewise be "disinterested." However, the trustee, with the judge's approval, may appoint an attorney who is not "disinterested" to prosecute special matters. "Disinterested," defined.

When is a trustee or his attorney, appointed by a judge in a reorganization proceeding, "disinter ested?" Section 158 of the Act answers this question as follows: 

A person is not deemed disinterested ifhe is a creditor or stockholder of the debtor corporation; or (1) (2) he is or within five years prior to the date of the filing of the petition was, an underwriter of securities for the corporation; or (3) he is or within two years prior to the date of the filing of the petition was, a director, officer or employee of the debtor corporation or any such underwriter, or 84

CORPORATE REORGANIZATION 

(4) an attorney for the debtor or such underwriter; or it appears that he has, for any reason, an interest, direct or indirect, adverse to the interests of any class. of creditors or stockholders. 

Termination of trustee's appointment or debtor's possession.

Where the indebtedness is less than $250,000, the judge may terminate the trustee's appointment at any time, and may restore the debtor to possession; or he may terminate the debtor's possession and appoint a trustee. In any case, the judge may appoint additional trustees or remove and substitute trustees. 

Hearing on objections to trustees or debtor in possession. 

Not less than thirty days nor more than sixty days after the approval of the petition, and upon not less than thirty days notice To creditors, stockholders, indenture trustees, the Securities and Exchange Commission and such other persons as the judge may designate, a hearing must be held at which the judge may hear objections to the continuance of a debtor in possession, or to the retention in office of a trustee, on the ground that he is not qualified, or that he is not disinterested.

Schedules. Upon the approval of the petition, the debtor corporation, where it is continued in possession, must at the expense of the estate and within the time fixed by the court, prepare, swear to and file in court, the following: (1) (2) (3) a schedule of its property, showing location, quantity and value; a schedule of its creditors of each class, showing the amounts and character of their claims and securities and, so far as known, the name and address or place of business of each creditor; and a schedule of its stockholders of each class, showing the number and kind of shares registered in the name of each stockholder, and his last-known address or place of business.

Where the debtor is not continued in possession, the trustee must file the information referred to in items (2) and (3) above. 

Lists of security holders and other information in the possession of third parties. Where lists or other information as to security holders are in the possession of third parties, such as stock brokers 85

BANKRUPTCY 

or similar agencies, and such information is necessary in order to disclose the true beneficial owners of the securities or in order to make up the schedules to be filed, the court may direct the production or inspection of such lists or information, or the furnishing of true copies thereof.

Access to information: impounding schedules, etc. The court may, for cause, order all schedules, lists, etc. impounded or kept in the custody of the court, and may permit their inspection by the trustee, or any indenture trustee, creditor, or stockholder, upon such terms as the court may prescribe. The court may deny inspection to any creditor or stockholder who acquired his claim or stock within three months prior to the filing of the petition or during the pendency of the proceeding. 

Trustee's duties. In addition to his other duties and responsibilities, including the duty of formulating a plan, the trustee is charged with the following specific duties: (1) Preliminary investigation and report to judge. Upon his appointment and qualification, the trustee must, if the judge so directs, investigate the acts, conduct, property, liabilities and financial condition of the debtor, the operation and desirability of continuing its business, and any other matters relevant to the proceeding or the formulation of a plan; and he must report thereon to the judge. (2) Examination of directors and officers. If the judge so directs, the trustee must examine the directors and officers of the debtor corporation, and any other witnesses. (3) Report on irregularities. The trustee must report to the judge any facts ascertained by him pertaining to fraud, misconduct, mismanagement and irregularities, and grounds for suit by the estate. (4) Employment of assistants. The trustee may employ such assistants as the judge may approve. (5) Report to creditors, stockholders, etc. At the earliest date practicable, the trustee must prepare and submit to creditors, stockholders, indenture trustees, the Securities and Exchange Commission and such other persons as the judge shall designate, a brief statement of his investigation of the property, liabilities and financial position of the debtor, the operation of its business and the desirability of continuing it. 86

CORPORATE REORGANIZATION 

(6) Inviting creditors and stockholders to submit suggestions for plan. The trustee must notify creditors and stockholders that they may submit, within a specified time, suggestions or proposals for a plan of reorganization. 

Appointment and duties of disinterested examiner, where debtor in possession. Where the debtor is in possession, the judge may at any time appoint a disinterested person as examiner to prepare and file a plan of reorganization, and to perform the duties otherwise imposed upon the trustee and listed in items (1) to (5) inclusive of the previous paragraph.

F. Formulation and Provisions of Plan 

Trustee's duty to formulate plan. Where a trustee has been appointed, the judge fixes a time within which the trustee must prepare and file a plan, or report his reasons why a plan cannot be effected.

Formulation of plan where debtor continued in possession. 

Where the debtor has been continued in possession, a plan or plans may be filed, within a time fixed by the judge- (1) by the debtor; (2) by any creditor or indenture trustee;

(3) (4) by any stockholder (if the debtor is found to be solvent); by the disinterested examiner, if so directed by the judge.

Mandatory provisions of plan. Among the mandatory provisions of a plan of reorganization are the following: (1) Modification in rights of creditors. The plan must include, as to creditors generally or as to some class of them, secured or unsecured, provisions altering or modifying their rights, either through the issuance of new securities, or otherwise. (2) Costs and administration expenses. The plan must provide for the payment of all costs, administration expenses and other allowances approved by the court. (3) Claims to be paid in cash. The plan must specify what claims, if any, are to be paid in cash in full. (4) Creditors and stockholders not affected by plan. The plan must specify the creditors or stockholders, or any class of 87

BANKRUPTCY 

them, not to be affected by the plan, and the provisions, if any, with respect to them. (5) Creditors who do not accept plan. The plan must provide for any class of creditors which is affected by, but does not accept the plan by the two-thirds vote required by the Act. 

The claims of such creditors as to property affected by the plan must be protected— (a) by transferring or selling such property, or by the debtor retaining it, subject to the claims of such creditors; or (b) by the sale of such property at a fair upset price, free of such claims; the proceeds of such sale, however, to be subject to such claims; or (c) by appraisal and payment in cash of the full value of such claims; or (d) by such other method as will be fair and equitable. (6) 

Stockholders who do not accept plan. Except where the judge determines that the debtor is insolvent, the plan must provide for any class of stockholders which is affected by, but does not accept the plan by the majority vote required by the Act. 

The equity, if any, of such stockholders in the property affected by the plan must be protected-- (a) (b) by the sale of such property at a fair upset price; or by appraisal and payment in cash of the value of their stock; or (c) by such other method as will be fair and equitable. (7) Execution of plan. The plan must provide adequate means for its execution, which may include: 

Retention by the debtor of all or any part of its property; (a) (b) the sale or transfer of all or any part of its property to one or more other corporations previously organized, or subsequently to be organized; (c) the merger or consolidation of the debtor with one or more other corporations; (d) the sale of all or any part of its property, either 88

CORPORATE REORGANIZATION 

subject to or free from any lien, at not less than a fair upset price, and the distribution of all or any part of the assets, or the proceeds derived from their sale, among those having an interest in them; (e) the satisfaction or modification of liens; (f) the cancellation or modification of indentures, or of other similar instruments; (g) (h) (i) (j) the curing or waiver of defaults; changes in the terms of outstanding securities, such as extending maturity dates or changing interest rates; amendment of the debtor's charter; issuance of securities of the debtor or other corporation, (1) for cash, (2) for property, (3) in exchange for existing securities, (4) in satisfaction of claims or stock, or (5) for other appropriate purposes. (8) Directors, officers or voting trustees. The plan must include equitable provisions consistent with public policy for the manner of selecting directors, officers or voting trustees, if any, upon the consummation of the plan, and for the selection of their successors. 

(9) Rights of new stockholders. The plan must provide for inclusion in the charter of the debtor or other corporation organized to carry out the plan, of provisions safeguarding the rights and privileges of stockholders, including: (a) Provisions prohibiting the issuance of non-voting stock; (b) Provisions for a fair and equitable distribution of power among the various classes of voting stockholders; (c) Fair, equitable and sound provisions governing the terms, position, rights and privileges of the various classes of securities, such as provisions with respect to the issuance, acquisition, purchase, retirement or redemption of such securities, the declaration of dividends, etc. 89

BANKRUPTCY 

(d) Where the indebtedness, liquidated as to amount and not contingent as to liability, is $250,000 or over, provisions for making periodic reports, at least annually, to security holders, including profit and loss statements and balance sheets. (10) Claims belonging to debtor. The plan must provide, as to claims belonging to the debtor or to the estate which are not settled or adjusted in the plan, that such claims be retained and enforced by the trustee, or, if the debtor has been continued in possession, by the disinterested examiner appointed for that purpose. 

Optional provisions of plan. Among the optional provisions of a plan of reorganization are the following: 

(1) Modification in rights of stockholders. The plan may include, as to stockholders generally, or some class of them, provisions altering or modifying their rights, either through the issuance of new securities, or otherwise. (2) Property affected. The plan may deal with all or any part of the debtor's property. (3) Rejection of executory contracts. The plan may provide for the rejection of any executory contract, except contracts in the public authority. (4) Retirement of long-term indebtedness. Where the plan creates or extends an indebtedness for a period of more than five years, it may provide for a sinking fund to retire such indebtedness. (5) Claims belonging to debtor or estate. The plan may include provisions for the settlement or adjustment of claims belonging to the debtor or the estate. (6) Other provisions. The plan may include any other provisions not inconsistent with the rest of the Act. 

G. Hearings on Plan: Approval by Judge 

Notice of hearing. Upon the filing of a plan by the trustee, or of any other plans where the debtor remains in possession, the judge fixes a time for a hearing on such plans, and for the consideration of any objections or amendments. Notice of such hearing must be given to the debtor, the creditors and stockholders, the indenture trustee, the Secretary of the Treasury, the Securities and Exchange Commission, and such other persons as the court may designate. 

Submission of plans to Securities and Exchange Commission.

After the hearing, and before approval of any plan, the judge may, 90

CORPORATE REORGANIZATION 

if the indebtedness does not exceed $3,000,000, and must, if it does, submit to the Securities and Exchange Commission, for examination or report, the plan or plans which he regards as worthy of consideration. The report of the Securities and Exchange Commission is advisory only. However, the judge must not approve a plan submitted to the Securities and Exchange Commission until after the latter has filed its report on it, or has notified the judge that it will not file a report, or until the time fixed by the judge for filing such report has expired.

Approval of plan by judge. After the hearing, and upon receiving the report of the Securities and Exchange Commission, or upon being advised that it will not file a report, or upon expiration of the time for filing such report, the judge enters an order approving such plan or plans as he deems fair, equitable and in conformity with the provisions prescribed in the Act. The order fixes a time within which creditors and stockholders affected by any plan must accept it.

H. Acceptance of Plan by Creditors and Stockholders 

Submission of information to creditors and stockholders.

Upon approval of a plan by the judge, the trustee or the debtor in possession, as the case may be, must transmit to all creditors and stockholders affected by the plan, the following information: (1) the plan or plans approved, together with a summary thereof approved by the judge; (2) the opinion of the judge, if any, approving the plan or plans, or a summary thereof approved by the judge; (3) the report, if any, filed by the Securities and Exchange

Commission, or a summary thereof prepared by the Commission; and (4) such other information as the judge deems desirable or necessary.

No solicitation of acceptances before order of approval. 

The Act (sec. 176) provides that "No person shall, without the consent of the court, solicit any acceptance, conditional or unconditional, of any plan, or any authority, conditional or unconditional, to accept any plan, whether by proxy, deposit, power of attorney or otherwise, until after the entry of an order approving such plan and the transmittal thereof to the creditors and stockholders, as provided in section 175 of this Act; and any such authority or acceptance given, procured, or received by reason of 91

BANKRUPTCY 

a solicitation prior to such approval and transmittal shall be invalid, unless such consent of the court has been so obtained." 

Approval of plan where debtor is a public utility corporation. 

Where the debtor is a public utility corporation subject to regulatory control by a commission, the latter body must be consulted prior to the approval of any plan. In the case of an intrastate utility, any plan affecting the public interest must first be approved by the corresponding state regulatory commission. 

Vote required for acceptance. 

Acceptance of a plan must be in writing, filed in court, by or on behalf of creditors holding twothirds in amount of the claims filed and allowed of each class, and, if the debtor is solvent, by or on behalf of stockholders holding a majority of stock, of which proofs have been filed, of each class; exclusive, in both cases, of creditors and stockholders not affected by the plan, or whose claims or stock are disqualified, or for whom other protection has been provided.

I. Confirmation of Plan by Court 

In general; notice of hearing. The judge must not only approve a plan before it is submitted to creditors and stockholders for acceptance, but he must also confirm it after it has been accepted. 

The judge fixes a date for a hearing on such confirmation, and of objections thereto, upon notice to the debtor, creditors, stockholders, indenture trustees, the Secretary of the Treasury, the Securities and Exchange Commission and such other persons as the judge may designate. The fact that the judge has previously approved the plan does not affect the right to object to its confirmation.

When judge confirms. The judge must confirm a plan if satisfied that- (1) The plan complies with the law; (2) The plan is fair, equitable and feasible; (3) The proposal of the plan and its acceptance are in good faith and have not been made or procured by means or promises forbidden by the Act; (4) All payments made or promised by the debtor or by a corporation issuing securities or acquiring property under the plan or by any other person, for services and for costs and expenses in, or in connection with, the proceeding or in connection with the plan and incident to the reorganization, have been fully disclosed to the judge and are reasonable or, if to be fixed after con- 92

CORPORATE REORGANIZATION 

firmation of the plan, will be subject to the approval of the judge; and (5) The identity, qualifications, and affiliations of the persons who are to be directors or officers, or voting trustees, if any, upon the consummation of the plan, have been fully disclosed, and that the appointment of such persons to such offices, or their continuance therein, is equitable, compatible with the interests of the creditors and stockholders and consistent with public policy. 

When confirmation to be refused: "crutches under corporate cripples." The provisions for corporate reorganization under the 

Bankruptcy Act of 1938 were designed to rehabilitate victims. of misfortune, but not to perpetuate their chronic weaknesses. In the language of the court in Price v. Spokane Silver & Lead Co., 97 Fed. (2d) 237 (C.C.A., S.D.), certiorari denied 305 U.S. 

626, Congress, in providing for corporate reorganization, did rot intend "to place crutches under corporate cripples, fit subjects for liquidation, and send them out into the business world to be a menace to all who might purchase their securities or deal with them on credit." The court goes on to lay down the following rules covering the confirmation of plans of reorganization, particularly with reference to the status of stockholders: (1) It is not enough to justify confirmation that a plan is feasible on its face, and that objecting creditors have failed to show that it is not feasible. A judge must satisfy himself on his own scrutiny that the plan is in all respects fair, equitable and feasible. (2) The court must be on guard against attempts to favor stockholders at the expense of creditors: the judge must scrutinize a plan to make sure assets belonging to creditors are not diverted to stockholders. (3) Stockholders are not ordinarily entitled to participate in a plan of reorganization if the debtor is clearly insolvent. (4) Stockholders, to justify their retention of a stock interest, must show either (a) that they have furnished consideration additional than that for which the stock was acquired, or (b) that they have an equity in the debtor's estate after the rights of creditors are fully provided for. (5) Where it is not shown that the proposed plan of reorganization was fair, equitable or feasible, or that stockholders had the right to participate as provided in the plan, the mere fact that a large majority of creditors have approved the plan is of no avail to sustain it. 93

BANKRUPTCY 

Alteration or modification of plan. A plan may be altered or modified, with the judge's approval, after it has been submitted for acceptance and either before or after its confirmation, provided such alteration or modification, in the judge's opinion, does not materially and adversely affect the interests of creditors or stockholders. If the judge finds that the proposed alteration or modification, filed with his approval, does materially and adversely affect the interests of creditors and stockholders, he must fix a hearing for the consideration of such alteration or modification, and a subsequent time for its acceptance or rejection. In such event, the 

Securities and Exchange Commission must be consulted, as in connection with the plan itself. Any creditor or stockholder who has previously accepted the plan and who does not file a written rejection of the proposed alteration or modification within the time fixed by the judge, is deemed to have accepted the alteration or modification.

J. Consummation of Plan 

Effect of confirmation. Upon confirmation by the court, the plan and its provisions are binding on the debtor, on every other corporation issuing securities or acquiring property under the plan, and upon all creditors and stockholders, whether or not such creditors and stockholders are affected by the plan, or have accepted it. 

The debtor and every other corporation organized or to be organized for the purpose of carrying out the plan must cooperate in carrying the plan through to execution. If the judge so directs, the monies for all payments provided for under the plan must be deposited and distributed as the court may direct. 

Hearing of objections to distribution. Any party in interest may object to a distribution by challenging the validity of any claim or interest filed and allowed, or listed by the trustee or scheduled by the debtor. The objection must be heard and summarily determined by the court.

Time limit for surrender of claims and securities. In carrying out a distribution, the judge may, on notice to all persons affected, fix a time, to expire not sooner than five years after the final decree closing the estate, within which creditors must file, assign, transfer or release their claims, and within which security holders must present or surrender their securities. After such time, no such claim. or stock can participate under the plan. 94

CORPORATE REORGANIZATION 

Unclaimed cash and securities. Securities or cash remaining unclaimed after the expiration of the time fixed by the court for the surrender of claims and securities, become the property of the debtor, or of the new corporation acquiring the assets of the debtor under the plan, free and clear of any and all claims and interests. 

Property transferred or retained, to be free of claims. All property of the debtor transferred or retained under the plan, is transferred or retained free of all claims and interests of the debtor, creditors and stockholders, except as otherwise provided by the plan or by court order. Such transfers or retentions relate to- (1) Transfer by the trustee to the debtor or other corporation or corporations provided for by the plan; or (2) Transfer by the debtor in possession to such other corporation or corporations; or (3) Retention by the debtor in possession.

Execution of instruments, satisfaction of liens, etc. The court may direct the execution and delivery of all instruments necessary to effectuate a transfer or retention of property in consummating the plan of reorganization. This applies to the debtor, its trustee, and any mortgagees, indenture trustees and any other parties who may have to join in the execution of instruments for the transfer of property or the satisfaction of liens. 

Final decree on consummation of plan. Upon the consummation of the plan, the judge must enter a final decree discharging the debtor from all its debts and liabilities; terminating all rights and interests of stockholders except as provided for under the plan; discharging the trustee, if any; making such provision by way of injunction or otherwise as may be necessary; and closing the estate. 

K. Dismissals and Adjudications 

It is conceivable 

Failure to effectuate or consummate plan. that a petition for reorganization may be filed, but may be rendered ineffective for any one of several reasons. It may be that no plan of reorganization is proposed within the time fixed by the judge. 

If a plan is proposed, it may not be approved by the judge. 

If a plan is approved by the judge, it may not be accepted by the creditors and stockholders; and even if a plan is accepted by the creditors and stockholders, it may not be confirmed by the court. 

In any such situation, what disposition must be made of the petition filed by the debtor? 95

BANKRUPTCY 

Where petition filed during bankruptcy proceeding. Where a petition was filed during the course of a bankruptcy proceeding and there has been a failure to effectuate or consummate a plan of reorganization, the judge must enter an order dismissing the reorganization proceeding and directing that the bankruptcy be proceeded with pursuant to the Act. 

Where petitioner was not in bankruptcy. 

Where there was no bankruptcy proceeding in existence at the time the petition was filed, the petition is either dismissed, or the petitioner adjudicated a bankrupt, as the facts may warrant. If the petitioner is adjudicated a bankrupt, the proceedings which follow are the same as if no petition for reorganization had been filed. In other words, we are then back to an ordinary bankruptcy proceeding. 96

CHAPTER 11 ARRANGEMENTS 

Definition and purpose: extension of debtor relief. An "arrangement" is defined by the Bankruptcy Act as "any plan of a debtor for the settlement, satisfaction, or extension of the time of payment of his unsecured debts, upon any terms." 

As pointed out in the initial chapter, Congress adopted a makeshift provision for the relief of debtors in 1933. This added a section to the Act which gave debtors an opportunity to work their way out of their financial difficulties by judicially controlled composition or extension agreements, thereby giving them a new business start without the stigma of bankruptcy. Still another section gave farmers a similar opportunity. These sections were superseded by the amendments of 1938, which extended and amplified the principle of debtor-relief.

Relief to ordinary debtors was provided by a new Chapter XI, entitled "Arrangements," governing unsecured debts only. 

Chapter XII of the new Act provided for "Real Property 

Arrangements," and thereby extended relief in respect to secured debts.

Chapter XIII of the new Act, entitled "Wage Earners' 

Plans," provided for plans to relieve wage earners from the hardships of garnishment and attachment proceedings, and employers from the annoyance to which they were subjected by reason of such proceedings.

We shall confine our attention to arrangements governing ordinary debtors, as set forth in Chapter XI of the Act. 

Origin and purpose of arrangements. Arrangements are really an outgrowth of compositions in bankruptcy, which were formerly provided for in section 12 of the Bankruptcy Act. When 

Congress in 1933 added the new sections providing for debtor relief, it allowed the old section 12 providing for compositions to remain in the Act. The result was considerable confusion, until both provisions for relief were superseded by Chapter XI of the Bankruptcy Act of 1938.

The basic purpose of an arrangement is the same as that 97

BANKRUPTCY 

which governed compositions under the old Act: to effect a com promise with unsecured creditors by offering them a settlement which would yield them as much benefit as a full proceeding in bankruptcy without, however, the delays and expense of such proceeding; and at the same time to enable the debtor, freed from his financial burdens, to resume his normal status and activities. 

Compositions and arrangements v. common-law settlements. 

Common-law or ordinary voluntary settlements between a debtor and his creditors have always labored under one fundamental handicap the power of a minority. to block a full settlement. 

Compositions in bankruptcy under the old Act and arrangements under the present Act were designed to overcome this difficulty. 

Under an ordinary settlement, a debtor calls in his creditors numbering, say, a hundred in all, and presents a proposal for paying them all off on a given percentage or time basis. The proposal is satisfactory to all but two or three, who insist on payment in full, or on other more favorable terms than those proposed. 

If their claims are ignored and a settlement is effected with the satisfied majority, the disgruntled minority may sue for the amounts due them in full or, worse yet, may file a petition in bankruptcy on the ground of preferential payments by the debtor while insolvent. On the other hand, to effect a secret arrangement with the dissatisfied few, a practice all too common, is itself not wholly free from risk. Neither is an assignment for the benefit of creditors under a state debtor-and-creditor statute: such an assignment, since it constitutes an act of bankruptcy, is all too frequently utilized as a club by minority creditors unwilling to abide by the will of the majority.

It is small wonder, therefore, that many debtors under the old Act preferred to incur the stigma of a voluntary petition with the hope of an early settlement by means of a composition. For by means of a composition in bankruptcy, a willing majority could override a rebellious minority. "Except for this coercion of the minority," as Judge Lowell observed in In re Lane, 125 Fed. 772, "the intervention of the court of bankruptcy would hardly be necessary."

This advantage has been carried over into arrangements: the minority must give way to the majority. The Act of 1938 thus embodies Judge Lowell's suggestion that except for coercion by a minority, the intervention by the bankruptcy court would hardly be necessary; because under the new Act one may petition for an arrangement without the brand of a bankruptcy proceeding. 98

ARRANGEMENTS 

Arrangements v. compositions. Although arrangements provide the same advantage as to minorities which was embodied in compositions, there is an essential difference between the two. A composition in bankruptcy, it is true, could be effected either before or after adjudication, but it had to be consummated within the framework of a bankruptcy court. An arrangement, on the other hand, may be effected either during the course of a bankruptcy proceeding, or without any proceeding in bankruptcy at all. 

Who may take advantage of arrangements. Any person who could become a bankrupt may file a petition for an arrangement. This includes individuals, partnerships and corporations. 

Chapter X of the Act, providing for corporate reorganizations, made no provision for individuals and partnerships; hence it was necessary to make separate provision for the latter. As noted in the previous chapter, the provisions for corporate reorganization under Chapter X do not preclude relief to corporations by way of an arrangement. Indeed, if a corporation is insolvent, and if all the debts to be adjusted are unsecured, so that its true purpose is to seek a compromise rather than a reorganization of its capital obligations, it must seek relief through an arrangement rather than through a reorganization. 

Insolvency as basis for arrangement. A debtor, to seek an arrangement, need not be "insolvent" under the definition of the 

Bankruptcy Act (excess of liabilities over assets). Insolvency in the equity or state meaning of the term (inability to pay debts as they mature) is sufficient. 

Summary of procedure for an arrangement. for an arrangement, in brief, is as follows:

The procedure 

1. A petition for an arrangement is filed by a debtor in the United States District Court, either during the pendency of a bankruptcy proceeding, or as an original petition without the pendency of such proceeding. 

2. The court thereupon takes jurisdiction of the debtor's property wherever located. 

3. The judge may: (a) refer the proceeding to a referee, (b) appoint a receiver, (c) continue the debtor in possession, or, (d) if a trustee has already been previously appointed, continue such trustee in possession. 

4. Creditors, who may be divided by the judge into classes, file their claims against the debtor, and either accept or reject the proposed arrangement. 99

BANKRUPTCY 

5. The plan of arrangement, upon being approved by the creditors, must be confirmed by the court. 

6. If an arrangement is withdrawn or abandoned prior to acceptance, or is not accepted within the time fixed by the court, or is not complied with, the proceeding may be dismissed. If the petition was filed during the course of a bankruptcy proceeding, such proceeding is continued as if no petition for an arrangement had been filed.

A. Jurisdiction, Powers and Duties of Court 

On the filing 

Exclusive jurisdiction of debtor and property. of a debtor's petition the court acquires exclusive jurisdiction of his property wherever located. This means that if a trustee is appointed in any District Court of any district in the United States he can go anywhere throughout the country, without the necessity of ancillary proceedings, and enforce the jurisdiction of his District Court in reference to any property of the debtor wherever he may find it.

Jurisdiction to administer estate. Upon the filing of a petition, the court, in addition to its other powers and duties, may permit the rejection of the debtor's executory contracts, and may authorize the receiver, trustee or debtor in possession to lease or sell any of the debtor's property, real or personal, upon such terms as the court may approve.

Stay of actions. We have already referred in a previous chapter to the automatic effect of the filing of a petition in bankruptcy in respect to suits pending in other courts. In addition to this power the court, upon the filing of a petition for an arrangement, may enjoin or stay the commencement or continuation of suits (other than suits to enforce liens upon the debtor's property) until a final decree is made in the arrangement proceeding. The court also has the power, upon notice and for cause shown, to enjoin or stay the commencement or continuation of any proceeding to enforce any lien upon the property of the debtor, until final decree in the arrangement proceeding. Where, however, the debtor files a petition for an arrangement during the course of a pending bankruptcy proceeding, such petition does not act automatically as a stay of adjudication or of administration of the estate. In such cases the court may, upon the application of the debtor, and upon notice to all parties in interest, grant a stay of adjudication or of admini- 100

ARRANGEMENTS 

stration of the estate, upon such terms as may be proper for the protection of the estate and to indemnify it against loss. 

B. Petition 

When may be filed. A debtor may file a petition for an arrangement either during the course of a bankruptcy proceeding, or without the pendency of such proceeding. In the former case, the petition may be filed either before or after an adjudication. 

Contents of petition. A petition for an arrangement must state that the debtor is insolvent or unable to pay his debts as they mature, and must set forth the provisions of the arrangement proposed by him. The petition must also be accompanied by: (1) a statement of the debtor's executory contracts; (2) the debtor's schedules and statement of affairs, if not previously filed; and (3) payment of fees, if not already paid. 

Provisions of arrangement. The proposed arrangement must include provisions modifying or altering the rights of unsecured creditors generally, or of some class of them, upon any terms or for any consideration. It may also include: (1) 

Provisions for treating unsecured debts on an equality with one another, or for the division of such debts into classes and their treatment in different ways or upon different terms; (2) Provisions for the rejection of any executory contract; (3) Provisions for specific undertakings by the debtor during any period of an extension of time provided for by the arrangement, including provisions for payments (4) on account;

Provisions for terminating any period of extension under specified conditions; (5) Provisions for continuing the debtor's business with or without supervision or control by a receiver or by a committee of creditors or otherwise; (6) Provision for payment of debts incurred after the filing of a petition and during the pendency of an arrangement, in priority over the debts affected by the arrangement; 101

BANKRUPTCY 

(7) Provisions for the court's retaining jurisdiction until the terms of the arrangement have been performed; and (8) Any other appropriate provisions not contrary to the 

Act. 

Bond of debtor in original petition. Where a petition is filed by an original debtor, that is, without the pendency of a bankruptcy proceeding, the court may order the debtor to file a bond or undertaking, with sureties to be approved, and in an amount to be fixed by the court, to indemnify the debtor's estate against subsequent loss or diminution until the entry of an order of adjudication in bankruptcy, should failure of the arrangement proceedings be followed by such adjudication. Should the debtor fail to file such bond, the court may either adjudge him a bankrupt, and direct that bankruptcy be proceeded with under the Act, or dismiss the proceedings, whichever course may seem to the court in the interest of the creditors.

C. Proceedings Subsequent to Filing of Petition 

Reference to referee. On the filing of a petition, the judge may refer the proceeding to a referee, who then acts as the court and has authority to conduct the proceedings and to make such orders as are not reserved to the judge himself.

Appointment of receiver, or continuance of trustee or debtor in possession. Upon application of any party in interest the court may, if necessary, appoint a receiver of the debtor's property. If a trustee in bankruptcy has previously been appointed, the court must continue such trustee in possession. Where no receiver or trustee is appointed, the debtor continues in possession of his property, retains title and exercises all the powers of a trustee, subject at all times to the control of the court. 

Appointment of appraisers. Upon the application of the receiver, trustee or any party in interest, the court may appoint one or more appraisers to prepare and file, under oath, an inventory and appraisal of the debtor's property. The court may prescribe how such inventory and appraisal shall be made. 

Meeting of creditors. Upon the filing of a petition, the court must promptly call a meeting of creditors upon at least ten days' notice by mail to creditors and other parties in interest. The notice must be accompanied by a copy of the proposed arrangement, a summary of the liabilities, as shown by the schedules, and a sum- 102

ARRANGEMENTS 

mary of the appraisal, if one has been made, or, if not, a summary of the assets as shown by the schedules. Such notice may also name the time for the filing of an application to confirm the arrangement, and a time for a hearing on such confirmation and any objections thereto. The judge or referee presides at such hearings, receives proofs of claim, allows or disallows them, examines the debtor or causes him to be examined, hears witnesses on any matter relevant to the proceeding and receives and determines the written acceptances of creditors on the proposed arrangement. 

Acceptances. The debtor is permitted to obtain acceptances to his proposed arrangement either before or after the filing of his petition. These acceptances, however, as already stated, are subject to approval by the judge or referee at the time of the hearing on the proposed arrangement. 

Proceedings following acceptance of arrangement. At the hearing for the purpose of passing upon the proposed arrangement and upon the acceptance thereof the court may direct the receiver or trustee, or, if none has been appointed, some other person, to receive and distribute the monies and consideration, if any, to be deposited by the debtor subject to the control of the court. The court must fix a time within which and a place where the debtor must deposit the consideration, if any, to be distributed to creditors, the money necessary to pay all debts having priority, and the money necessary to pay costs, disbursements and expenses of the proceeding. The court must also fix the time for the filing of an application to confirm the arrangement, and for a hearing on such confirmation, and on any objections thereto, unless, as pointed out in a previous paragraph, such time has already been fixed in the previous notice of the meeting called to pass upon the arrangement, or unless all creditors affected by the arrangement have accepted it. 

Apointment of creditors' committee and nomination of trustee. 

At the meeting called for the purpose of passing upon the proposed arrangement, the creditors may appoint a committee, if none has been previously appointed in a pending bankruptcy proceeding. 

If a trustee has not been previously appointed, the creditors may also nominate a trustee to be appointed by the court in case the arrangement proceedings fail and it becomes necessary to administer the estate in bankruptcy.

Conduct of business. The receiver, trustee or debtor in possession, as the case may be, has the power, upon authorization by the court and subject to its control, to operate the debtor's business and to manage his property for such period as the court may fix. 103

BANKRUPTCY 

During such operation or management the receiver, trustee or debtor in possession must report to the court on the conduct of the business at such intervals as the court may designate. 

D. Creditors and Claims 

Classification of creditors. For the purposes of an arrangement and its acceptance, the court may divide creditors into classes. 

All controversies in this connection must be heard and summarily determined by the court.

Rights, duties and liabilities of creditors. The rights, duties and liabilities of creditors and others in respect to the debtor's property are the same as in the case of a bankruptcy proceeding upon adjudication. 

Rejection of executory contract or unexpired lease. Mention was made in the preceding chapter of the right to reject executory contracts and leases in connection with corporate reorganizations. 

The Act of 1938 created the same right in respect to arrangements. 

The court has held that the right to reject executory contracts exists even where such contracts provide that they shall be unaffected by bankruptcy proceedings. In re Greenpoint Metallic Bed 

Co., 31 Fed. Supp. 923 (D.C., N.Y., 1940).

E. Confirmation of Arrangement 

Where arrangement unanimously accepted. An arrangement must be confirmed by the court, where (1) it has been accepted in writing at a creditors' meeting by all the creditors concerned, whether or not their claims have been proved, (2) the debtor has made the required deposit, and (3) the court is satisfied that the arrangement and its acceptance are in good faith and have not been made or procured by any forbidden means, promises or acts. 

Where arrangement not unanimously accepted. Where an arrangement has not been unanimously accepted, an application for confirmation may be filed with the court within such time as the court shall fix, under the following conditions: (1) The arrangement must have been accepted in writing by a majority in number of all creditors affected by it whose claims have been proved and allowed, or, if the creditors are divided into classes, by a majority in number of all creditors of each class. Such majority in number must also represent a majority in amount of 104

ARRANGEMENTS 

claims, either generally, or by each class, as the case may be. (2) The debtor must have made the required deposit.

Requisites for confirmation. The court must confirm an arrangement if satisfied that-- (1) (2) 

The law has been complied with;

The arrangement is fair, equitable and feasible, and is for the best interests of the creditors; (3) The debtor has not been guilty of any of the acts or failed to perform any of the duties which would be a bar to the discharge of a bankrupt; and (4) The proposal and its acceptance are in good faith and have not been made or procured by any forbidden means, promises or acts. 

When confirmation must be refused and petition dismissed. 

Where it is obvious that the petition has been filed in bad faith, the court must withhold confirmation of the proposed arrangement, and upon objection by any creditor, must dismiss the petition. Where the debtor has received a discharge in bankruptcy within six years, confirmation of a plan in a relief proceeding will be denied. 

In Hill v. Topeka Morris Plan Co., 105 Fed. (2d) 299 (C. 

C.A., Kan., 1939), certiorari denied 308 U.S. 595, the debtor filed a petition for a composition or extension of debts on July 16, 1938. On that date the court entered two orders, one approving the petition, and the other referring the matter to a referee in bankruptcy. "The referee," as stated in the opinion of the Circuit 

Court of Appeals, "found the petitioner had no assets; that he had filed a poverty affidavit with his petition, and that this is the fifth bankruptcy petition filed by the petitioner within the last twelve years; that on October 20, 1926, petitioner filed a petition in bankruptcy and was discharged on July 14, 1928; that on 

December 4, 1929, he filed another petition in bankruptcy but did not seek a discharge; that on January 30, 1931, petitioner filed another petition in bankruptcy, which was dismissed; that in June, 1933, the petitioner filed another petition in bankruptcy and was discharged February 18, 1936, as to certain debts (but not to appellee's debt). The referee further found that the petitioner cannot have another discharge until six years have elapsed from February 18, 1936; that the creditors appearing before the referee have refused to agree to any composition or extension and the bankrupt has no funds with which to effect a composition ***

At the hearing in this court it was admitted that one of the pur- 105

BANKRUPTCY
